Zooid : a DSL for Certified Multiparty Computation Journal: PACMPL CONF CCS: Software and its engineering General programming languages CCS: Social and professional topics History of programming languages
David Castro-Perez, Francisco Ferreira, Lorenzo Gheri, Nobuko Yoshida

Abstract
. We design and implement Zooid , a domain specific language for certified multiparty communication, embedded in Coq and implemented atop our mechanisation framework of asynchronous multiparty session types (the first of its kind). Zooid provides a fully mechanised metatheory for the semantics of global and local types, and a fully verified end-point process language that faithfully reflects the type-level behaviours and thus inherits the global types properties such as deadlock freedom, protocol compliance, and liveness guarantees.



From Mechanised Metatheory to Certified Multiparty Processes

(long version)

1.
Introduction

Concurrent behavioural type
systems

(
Hüttel et al. 2016
)
accurately simulate and abstract
the behaviour of interactive
processes
, as opposed to sequential types for
programs that simply describe values. The
session types
system

(
Honda 1993
;
Honda
et al. 1998
;
Takeuchi
et al. 1994
)
is one of such behavioural
type systems, which can determine protocol
compliance for processes. Session types consist of actions for sending and receiving, sequencing, choices, and
recursion. In session types, when a typed process communicates, its
type also evolves, thus reflecting the progression
of the state of the protocol (type) after
performing an action. This rich behavioural aspect of session types
has opened new areas of study, such as a connection with communicating
automata
(
Brand and
Zafiropulo 1983
)
and concurrent game semantics
(
Rideau and
Winskel 2011
)
by
linking actions of session types to transitions of state machines

(
Deniélou and
Yoshida 2012
)
and events of games
(
Castellan and
Yoshida 2019
)
.

Originally, binary session types (BST) provide deadlock-freedom for a pair of
processes, but not when more than two
participants
(often also called

roles
) are involved. For more than two processes,
ensuring deadlock-freedom in BST requires either complicated
additional causality-based typing systems on top of plain BST,
e.g.
(
Dezani-Ciancaglini et al. 2008
;
Balzer
et al. 2019
)
or
limitation to deterministic, strongly-normalising session types

(
Toninho and
Yoshida 2018a
;
Toninho and
Yoshida 2018b
)
.

Multiparty session types

(MPST,
(
Honda
et al. 2008
;
Honda
et al. 2016
)
) solve this limitation, by
defining
global types
as an overall specification of all the
communications by every participant involved. The essence of the MPST
theory (depicted in Figure
1
) is
end-point projection
where a global type
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is projected
into one
local type

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}{}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
for each participant, so that the
participant
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{proc}}{}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
can be implemented following an abstract behaviour
represented by the local type.
To ensure correctness, the collection of behaviours of the local types projected
from a global type need to mirror the behaviour of that global type.

\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}{}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}{}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\dots}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}{}_{n}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{proc}}{}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{proc}}{}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
\dots
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{proc}}{}_{n}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
projection (
\upharpoonright
)
typing (
\vdash_{\text{{lt}}}
)

Figure 1
.
MPST in a nutshell

The behaviour of global and local types is defined by
(asynchronous) labelled transition systems (LTS) whose sound and
complete correspondence is key to provide: progress of
processes
(
Honda
et al. 2016
)
, synthesis of global
protocols
(
Deniélou and
Yoshida 2013
;
Lange
et al. 2015
)
, and to establish bisimulation for
processes
(
Kouzapas and
Yoshida 2015
)
. Practically, type-level transition systems
are particularly useful for, e.g., dynamic monitoring of components
in distributed systems
(
Demangeon et al. 2015
)
and generating deadlock-free APIs
of various programming
languages, e.g.,

(
Castro
et al. 2019b
;
Scalas
et al. 2017
;
Hu and Yoshida 2017
;
Neykova
et al. 2018
;
Zhou
et al. 2020
)
.

Unfortunately, the more complicated the behaviour is,
the more
error-prone
the theory becomes.
The literature reveals broken proofs of subject reduction for several
MPST systems
(
Scalas and
Yoshida 2019
)
, and a flaw of the decidability of
subtyping
(
Bravetti
et al. 2017
)
for asynchronous MPST. All of which are
caused by an incorrect understanding of the (asynchronous) behaviour of
types.

Motivated by this experience, we design and implement

Zooid

1
1

1

A
zooid

is a single animal that is part of a colonial animal, akin to how an
endpoint process is part of a distributed system.
, a certified
Domain Specific Language (DSL) to write
well-typed by
construction
communicating processes.
Zooid
’s
implementation is embedded in the Coq proof
assistant
(
The Coq
Development Team 2020
)
, so that it relies on solid and precise
foundations: in Coq we have formalised the metatheory for MPST, which
serves as the type system for
Zooid
. On one side, mechanising the
metatheory is immediately useful for documenting, clarifying, and
ensuring the validity of proofs, on the other it results in certified
specifications and implementations of the concepts in the theory.

Zooid
exemplifies this for MPST, a complex and relevant theory
with many real-world applications. In this system, not only the theory
is validated in Coq, the actual implementation of projection, type
checking and validation of processes, is extracted from certified
proofs.

We provide the first
fully mechanised
proof of sound and
complete correspondence between the labelled transition systems of
global and local types, in terms of equivalence of execution traces,
recapturing the original LTS provided in
(
Deniélou and
Yoshida 2013
)
.
In this work, instead of trying to formalise existing proofs in the
literature, we approach the problem with a fresh look and use
tools that would allow for a successful and reusable mechanisation. On
the theory side, we use coinductive trees inspired
by
(
Xia et al. 2019
;
Ghilezan et al. 2019
)
; on the tool side, we
depend on the Coq proof assistant
(
The Coq
Development Team 2020
)
, taking advantage
of small scale reflection (SSReflect)
(
Gonthier and
Mahboubi 2010
)
to
structure our proofs, and PaCo
(
Hur
et al. 2013
)
to provide a powerful
parameterised coinduction library, which we use extensively.

To certify an MPST end-point process implementation,
we define a concurrent process language and an
LTS semantics for it. This guarantees that process traces
respect the ones from its local and global types.
Naturally, processes do not need to implement every aspect of the
protocol. Therefore, we define the notion of complete subtraces to
represent the fact that an implementation may choose not to implement
some aspects, but it still needs to match the global trace (we make precise
this concept in §
4.3
). Our final result
is the design and implementation of
Zooid
, a Coq-embedded
DSL to write end-point processes that are
well-typed (hence deadlock-free and live)
by construction
.
This development takes full
advantage of the metatheory to provide a certified validation,
projection, and type checking for
Zooid
processes.

The contributions of this work are fourfold:

Fully mechanised transition systems:

for global and local types,
using
asynchronous communications
and proofs of their sound and
complete trace equivalence.

Semantic representation:

of behavioural
types based on
coinductive trees
,
proposing a novel approach to the proof of trace
equivalences.

A concurrent process language:

with an associated typing discipline and the
notion of
complete subtraces
to relate process traces to global
traces, as processes may not fully implement a protocol and still be
compliant.

Zooid
:

a DSL embedded in Coq and framework that

specifies
global protocols, performs
projections
, and
implements intrinsically well-typed processes, using code
certified

by Coq proofs. The code of
Zooid
processes is
extracted into
OCaml
code for execution.
Zooid
uses the mechanisation to
provide a framework for processes that enjoy
deadlock freedom and liveness (with a type checker certified in Coq).

Outline.

In §
2
, we provide an overview
of the theory and the paper. In
§
3
, we present the theory of MPST together with the
soundness and completeness results. We describe the process language, its metatheory and the
Zooid
DSL
in §
4
. In §
5
, we present

Zooid
’s workflow and showcase its use with some examples.
In §
6
we discuss related work and offer
some future work and conclusions.

The git repository of our development is publicly available:
https://github.com/emtst/zooid-cmpst
; it contains all the
complete Coq definitions and proofs from the paper, together with the
examples and case studies implemented using
Zooid
.
In the Appendix, we present the proofs of our theorems (§
A
), and additional technical details of the toolchain (§
B
)
.

Figure 2
.
Our contribution at a glance.

In this section, we present our formalised results and
the relationship that puts them together to build
Zooid
; and we show,
with an example, how our development allows to
certify

the implementation of a multiparty protocol.

2.1.
Results and Development

Figure
2

summarises our contribution.
The yellow
rectangle on the background encases the metatheory that we have
formalised for types and processes. On such solid basis,
we build
Zooid
, our language for specifying end-point processes.

Types as Trees, Projection and Unravelling.

We formalise in Coq the inductive syntaxes of global types
and local types.
Of these,
we give an alternative representation in terms of coinductive trees,
moving one step forward towards semantics. By defining
the unravelling relation
\Re
, of a type into a tree (§
3.1
),
and projections
\upharpoonright
, from
global to local objects (§
3.2
), we
prove Theorem
3.6
:
projection is preserved by unravelling (square
(M.1)

in Figure
2
).

Trace Semantics.

Moving further to
the right, we define labelled
transition systems for trees
(§
3.3
and

3.4
). Exploiting their tree representation,
we give an asynchronous semantics in terms of execution traces
to global and local types (§
3.5
).

Soundness and completeness
come together
in the
trace equivalence theorem
for global
and local types, Theorem
3.21
, thus closing square

(M.2)
in Figure
2
.

Process Language and Typing.

We formalise the syntax for specifying (core) processes,
proc
in
Figure
2
(§
4.1
). We define a typing relation between local types and
processes, then we give semantics to processes (§
4.3
), again in terms of an LTS and execution traces,
and finally we prove type preservation, Theorem

4.5
. We conclude the metatheory part
with Theorem

4.7
, (thus closing square
(M.3)
of Figure
2
):
we show that
process traces are global traces
.

2.2.
Process Language:
Zooid

On the foundations of a formalised metatheory, we build a domain
specific language embedded in Coq,
Zooid
, as presented in §
4
and
5
. Processes specified in
Zooid
are well-typed by construction.
Zooid
terms are dependent pairs
of a core process
proc
, and a proof that it is
well-typed with respect to a given local type
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, obtained via
projection of the global type
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
given for the protocol.
Zooid

terms are built using a collection of
smart constructors
: we
make sure that the local type of any smart constructor is fully
determined by its inputs, so that we can use Coq to infer the local
type for every
Zooid
process.

To summarise, our end product
Zooid
is a DSL embedded in Coq. The user specifies as inputs:

(1)

the general discipline of the protocol as a global type;

(2)

the communicating process they are interested in, as a
Zooid
term.

From this the user will obtain:

(a)

a collection of local types inferred by projection from the given global type;

(b)

that their process is well-typed by construction;

(c)

a certified semantics for their process, namely the guarantee that the behaviour of their process adheres to the semantics of the global protocol.

Moreover the user’s process is easily translated to an OCaml program, thanks to Coq code-extraction.

2.3.
Zooid
at Work

We briefly illustrate how
Zooid
works with a simple example, a
ring protocol. We want to write a certified process for
{{\sf Alice}}
that
sends a message to
{{\sf Bob}}
and then receives a message from
{{\sf Carol}}
,
but only after
{{\sf Bob}}
and
{{\sf Carol}}
have exchanged a message themselves.
In what follows, all the considered messages are natural numbers
of type
nat
.

First, we provide
Zooid
with the intended disciplining protocol,
a global type
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
:

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\;}l@{}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\sf Alice}}\to{{\sf Bob}}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\ell(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}).}&\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\sf Bob}}\to{{\sf Carol}}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\ell(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}).}\\
&\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\sf Carol}}\to{{\sf Alice}}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\ell(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}).}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mathtt{end}}\end{array}

The global type
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
prescribes the full protocol, where
{{\sf Alice}}

sends a message containing a
nat
number to
{{\sf Bob}}
(with a
generic label
\ell
),
{{\sf Bob}}
receives it and sends another number
to
{{\sf Carol}}
, who receives and can send the last message to
{{\sf Alice}}
.

{{\sf Alice}}
receives and the protocol terminates (
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
).

Taking the point of view of
{{\sf Alice}}
, we automatically obtain
a local type
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
,
projection
of
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
onto the role
{{\sf Alice}}
:

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{![{{{\sf Bob}}}];}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\ell(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}).}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{?[{{{\sf Carol}}}];}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\ell(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
,

which prescribes for
{{\sf Alice}}
that she will send a number to
{{\sf Bob}}
,
receive a number from
{{\sf Carol}}
and terminate.

A
Zooid
implementation for
{{\sf Alice}}
’s process,
respecting
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, is (
{{\sf Alice}}
sends
x
to
{{\sf Bob}}
and
gets
y
from
{{\sf Carol}}
):

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{\;}l@{}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{proc}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=&\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{send}\;{{\sf Bob}}\;(\ell,\;x:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0})\texttt{!}\;}\\
&\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{recv}\;{{\sf Carol}}\;(\ell,\;\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{y}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0})\texttt{?}\;}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{finish}}\end{array}

Thanks to
Zooid
’s smart constructors, we obtain that

proc
is
well-typed
with respect to the local type
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
.
Additionally, the underlying metatheory certifies, by Coq
proofs, that the behaviour of
proc
conforms to the semantics of protocol
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
.

3.
Sound and Complete Asynchronous Multiparty Session Types

In this section, we describe the first layer of
Zooid
’s certified
development: a mechanisation of the metatheory of multiparty session types. We
focus on the design, main concepts and results, while for
a more in-detail presentation with pointers to the Coq mechanisation,
we refer to Appendix
A
.

3.1.
Global and Local Types

A
global type
describes the communication protocol in its entirety,
recording all the interactions between the different participants. Each
participant has a
local type
specifying its intended behaviour within the
protocol. The literature offers a wide variety of presentations of global and
local types
(
Honda
et al. 2008
;
Honda
et al. 2016
;
Scalas
et al. 2019
;
Coppo et al. 2015
)
:
here, building on

(
Deniélou and
Yoshida 2013
)
, we formalise full
asynchronous multiparty
session types
(MPST), which captures asynchronous communication,
with choice and recursion.

Definition 3.1 (Sorts, global and local types).

Sorts
(
mty
in
Common/AtomSets.v
),

global types
(
g_ty
in
Global/Syntax.v
), and

Local types
(
l_ty
in
Local/Syntax.v
), ranged over by
S
,
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, and
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
respectively,
are generated by:

\begin{array}[]{@{}r@{\;}l@{}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}&\Coloneqq\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbin{\mathbf{|\!\!|}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{int}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbin{\mathbf{|\!\!|}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{bool}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbin{\mathbf{|\!\!|}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\texttt{+}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbin{\mathbf{|\!\!|}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\texttt{*}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\[2.84526pt]
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}&\Coloneqq\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\mathbin{\mathbf{|\!\!|}}\;\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\mathbin{\mathbf{|\!\!|}}\;\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\mathbin{\mathbf{|\!\!|}}\;\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf p}\to{\sf q}:\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\\[2.84526pt]
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}&\Coloneqq\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbin{\mathbf{|\!\!|}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbin{\mathbf{|\!\!|}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mu\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}.\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbin{\mathbf{|\!\!|}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{![{{\sf q}}];\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbin{\mathbf{|\!\!|}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{?[{{\sf p}}];\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\end{array}

with
{\sf p}\neq{\sf q}
,
I\neq\emptyset,
and

\ell_{i}\neq\ell_{j}
when
i\neq j,
for all
i,j\in I
.

Above,
sorts
refer to the
types of supported message payloads.
We are interested in types such that (1) bound variables are

guarded
—e.g.,

\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}.\allowbreak\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf p}\to{\sf q}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\ell(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}).\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is a valid
global type, whereas
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{.}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is not—and (2) types are

closed
, i.e., all variables are bound by
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

(Appendix
A
, Definitions
A.2
and
A.3
).

In the literature, it is common to adopt the

equi-recursive viewpoint

(
Pierce 2002
)
, i.e., to identify

\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}/\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, given that
their intended behaviour is the same. Such unravelling of
recursion can be performed infinitely many times, thus
obtaining possibly infinite trees
2
2

2

Formally, in Coq, a coinductively defined datatype (codatatype)
of finitely branching trees with possible infinite depth.
,
whose structure derives from the
syntax of global and local types
(
Ghilezan et al. 2019
)
.

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

with

and

\ell_{i}\neq\ell_{j}
when
i\neq j,
for all

.

Global and local objects share the
type for a terminated protocol
\mathtt{end}
,
the injection of a variable
X
,
and the recursion construct
\mu X.\dots
; semantic global/local
trees do not include the last two constructs,
since recursion is captured by infinite depth
(Appendix
A.1
and
A.2
).

Global messages:

\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf p}\to{\sf q}:\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
describes a protocol where participant
{\sf p}
sends to
{\sf q}
one message with label
\ell_{i}
and a value of sort
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}{}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
as payload, for
some
i\in I
; then, depending on which
\ell_{i}
was sent by
{\sf p}
, the protocol continues as
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}{}_{i}
. With trees, we make explicit
the two asynchronous stages of the communication of a
message:
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf p}\to{\sf q}:\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
represents the status where a
message from
{\sf p}
to
{\sf q}
has yet to
be sent;
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf p}\mathrel{\hbox to12.19pt{\vbox to13.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.09431pt\lower-8.3694pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

Local messages:

send type

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{![{{\sf q}}];\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
:
the participant sends a message to
{\sf q}
; if the participant chooses the label
\ell_{i}
, then the sent payload value must be of sort

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}{}_{i}
, and it continues as prescribed by
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}{}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
.
Receive type

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{?[{{\sf p}}];\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
: the participant waits to receive from
{\sf p}
a value of sort
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}{}_{i}
, for some
i\in I
, via a message with label
\ell_{i}
; then the protocol continues
as prescribed by
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}{}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
. The same intuition holds, mutatis mutandis, for trees.

We define the function
prts
to return the set of participants (or

roles
) of a global type; e.g.
{\sf p}
and
{\sf q}
above. For global trees, we
define the predicate
part_of
. The formal definitions can be found in
Appendix
A.1
.

We formalise equi-recursion by relating
types with their representation as trees, as follows:

L^{c}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}$}\crcr}}&\hskip-5.69054pt\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\dfrac{\forall i\in I.\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}{}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\Re\;\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{{\sf L^{c}}}{}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\phantom{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{?[{{\sf p}}];\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\Re\;\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{?^{\sf c}[{{\sf p}}];\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{{\sf L^{c}}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}$\cr\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\dfrac{\phantom{\forall i\in I.\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}{}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\Re\;\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{{\sf L^{c}}}{}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{?[{{\sf p}}];\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\Re\;\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{?^{\sf c}[{{\sf p}}];\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{{\sf L^{c}}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}$}\crcr}}\end{array}\end{array}

Representing types in terms of trees
allows for a smoother mechanisation of the semantics.
The unravelling operation formally relates the two representations.

3.2.
Projections, or how to discipline communication

Projection
is the key operation of
multiparty session types: it extracts a local perspective of the protocol, from
the point of view of a single participant, from the global bird’s-eye
perspective offered by global types. We define both inductive and
coinductive projections.

\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\begin{array}[]{@{}lll@{}}\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5469}{\DefTirNameOld{\footnotesize[{proj-end}]}}&\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5469}{\DefTirNameOld{\footnotesize[{proj-send}]}}&\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5469}{\DefTirNameOld{\footnotesize[{proj-recv}]}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\upharpoonright}{{\sf r}}=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{end}}&{\sf r}={\sf p}\ \ \text{implies}\ \ {\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf p}\to{\sf q}:\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\upharpoonright}{{\sf r}}=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{![{{\sf q}}];\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}}_{i}).{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}{}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}{\upharpoonright}{{\sf r}}}\}_{i\in I}}&{\sf r}={\sf q}\ \ \text{implies}\ \ {\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf p}\to{\sf q}:\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\upharpoonright}{{\sf r}}=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{?[{{\sf p}}];\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}}_{i}).{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}{}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}{\upharpoonright}{{\sf r}}}\}_{i\in I}}\end{array}\\[5.69054pt]
\begin{array}[]{lll}\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5469}{\DefTirNameOld{\footnotesize[{proj-var}]}}&\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5469}{\DefTirNameOld{\footnotesize[{proj-rec}]}}&\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5469}{\DefTirNameOld{\footnotesize[{proj-cont}]}}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\upharpoonright}{{\sf r}}=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}&{(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}{\upharpoonright}{{\sf r}}=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mu\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}.}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\upharpoonright}{{\sf r}})\ \text{if}\ \ \texttt{guarded}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\upharpoonright}{{\sf r}})&\text{${\sf r}\neq{\sf p}$, ${\sf r}\neq{\sf q}$ and $\forall i,j\in I$, ${\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}{}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{i}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}{\upharpoonright}{{\sf r}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}{}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{j}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}{\upharpoonright}{{\sf r}}$ implies}\ \ {\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf p}\to{\sf q}:\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\upharpoonright}{{\sf r}}={\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}{}_{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\bar{\imath}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}{\upharpoonright}{{\sf r}}\ \ \text{(with $\bar{\imath}\in I$)}\end{array}\end{array}

(a)

I$)}}}$\cr\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\dfrac{\phantom{{\sf r}\neq{\sf p}\ \ \ {\sf r}\neq{\sf q}\ \ \ \forall i\in I.\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf L^{c}}}{}_{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{i}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}=\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{{\sf L^{c}}}{}_{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{j}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\ \ \ \forall i\in I$)}}$}\crcr}}&\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\dfrac{\neg\ \texttt{part\_of}\ {{\sf r}}\ {\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}{\phantom{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf {\upharpoonright^{\texttt{c}}}{{\sf r}}\ \color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{\mathtt{end}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}$}\crcr}}\end{array}\end{array}

(b)

Rules for coinductive projection, Definition
3.4

Figure 3
.
Projection rules

Definition 3.4.

The inductive
projection of a global type onto a participant
{\sf r}
(
project
in

Projection/IProject.v
) is a partial function

{\small{\_}{\upharpoonright}{{\sf r}}:\texttt{g\_ty}\nrightarrow\texttt{l\_ty}}

defined by recursion on
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
whenever one of the clauses in
Figure
3(a)
applies and the recursive call is defined;
the coinductive
projection of a global tree onto a participant
{\sf r}
(definitions
Project
and
IProj
in
Projection/CProject.v
) is a relation

{\small\_\ {\upharpoonright^{\texttt{c}}}{{\sf r}}\ \_:\texttt{rel}\ \ {\hskip-1.42262pt\texttt{g\_ty}^{\texttt{c}}}\ \ {\hskip-1.42262pt\texttt{l\_ty}^{\texttt{c}}}}
coinductively
defined in Figure
3(b)
.

In rules
\DefTirNameOld
[co-proj-end]
and
\DefTirNameOld
[co-proj-cont]
we have
added explicit conditions on participants. By factoring in the
predicate
part_of
, Definition
3.4
ensures (1) that the
projection of a global tree on a participant outside the protocol is

\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{\mathtt{end}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
(rule
\DefTirNameOld
[co-proj-end]
) and (2) that this discipline
is preserved in the continuations (rule
\DefTirNameOld
[co-proj-cont]
). We see
that the clauses for projecting of types and trees follow the same
intuition: projecting a global object onto a sending (resp. receiving) role gives a
sending (resp. receiving) local object, provided that the local continuations
are also projections of the corresponding global continuations. As expected,
the tree projection takes care explicitly of
asynchronicity (rules
\DefTirNameOld
[co-proj-send-2]
and
\DefTirNameOld
[co-proj-recv-2]
).
This is an adaptation to our coinductive setting of the
definition in
(
Deniélou and
Yoshida 2013
, Appendix A.1)
. Below we give an example
to clarify the meaning of
\DefTirNameOld
[proj-cont]
.

Example 3.5 (Projection).

About rule
\DefTirNameOld
[proj-cont]
, we observe that the type

\small\begin{array}[]{lll}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}{}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\sf Alice}}\to{{\sf Bob}}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\{}&\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\ell_{1}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}).}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\sf Bob}}\to{{\sf Carol}}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\ell(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}).\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5},}&\\
&\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\ell_{2}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}).\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\sf Alice}}\to{{\sf Carol}}:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\allowbreak\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\ell(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}).\allowbreak\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}&\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\}}\end{array}

is not projectable onto
Carol
, since, after skipping the first interaction between

Alice
and
Bob
, it would not be clear whether
Carol
should
expect a message
from
Alice
or from
Bob
. If we take instead

\small\begin{array}[]{lll}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\sf Alice}}\to{{\sf Bob}}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\{}&\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\ell_{1}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}).\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\sf Bob}}\to{{\sf Carol}}:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\ell(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}).\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5},}&\\
&\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\ell_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{bool}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5})}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\sf Bob}}\to{{\sf Carol}}:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\ell(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}).\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}&\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{},\par\end{array}

the projection
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\upharpoonright}{{{\sf Carol}}}
is well defined as the local type

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{?[{{{\sf Bob}}}];}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\allowbreak\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\ell(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
. Following common
practice, we use an option type to encode projection as
a partial function in Coq.

Coinductive projection is more permissive than its inductive
counterpart, since it removes the technical issues related to formally
dealing with (equi)recursion, thus allowing for a smoother development in Coq (Appendix
A.3

and
(
Ghilezan et al. 2019
, Definition 3.6 and Remark 3.14)
).

If, when reasoning about semantics, coinductive trees are
more convenient objects to work with, we still want to rely
on session types for imposing a typing discipline on
the communication. The followng theorem allows us to do so.

Theorem 3.6 (Unravelling preserves projections).

(
ic_proj
in
Projection/Correctness.v
.)
Given a global type
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, such that
\texttt{guarded}\ \color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and
\texttt{closed}\ \color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, if

(a)

there exists a local type
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\upharpoonright}{{\sf r}}=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\
,

(b)

there exists a global tree
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
such that
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\Re\;\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and,

(c)

there exists a local tree
\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{{\sf L^{c}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
such that
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\Re\;\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{{\sf L^{c}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
,

then
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ {\upharpoonright^{\texttt{c}}}{{\sf r}}\ \color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{{\sf L^{c}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
.

This first central result closes the first metatheory square
(M.1)

of
the diagram
in Figure
2
. For a sketch of its proof
see
Appendix
A
, Theorem
A.20
.

3.3.
Projection Environments for Asynchronous Communication

In this subsection, we introduce key concepts for building an
asynchronous operational semantics for MPST.
In
(
Deniélou and
Yoshida 2013
)
a precise correspondence is drawn
between communicating finite-state
automata and MPST. We do not formalise an explicit
syntax for automata, but develop labelled transition systems for
global and local trees with automata in mind.

Consider the following scenario:

{\sf p}
sends a message to
{\sf q}

with label
\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{\ell}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and payload of sort
S
and continues on

\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{{\sf L^{c}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, and dually
{\sf q}
receives from
{\sf p}
the message, with same
label and payload, and then continues on
\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{{\sf L^{c}}}{}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
.
For
{\sf q}
to receive the message, it is necessary that
{\sf p}
has first
sent it. To model this asynchronous behaviour, we use FIFO
queues: in the designated queue
Q({\sf p},{\sf q})
(empty at first) we
enqueue the message sent from
{\sf p}
, until the
message is received by
{\sf q}
and removed from the queue.
We use one queue for each ordered
pair of participants
({\sf p},{\sf q})
to store in-transit messages sent
from
{\sf p}
to
{\sf q}
, and we collect such queues in
queue environments
.

Definition 3.7 (Queue environments).

We call
queue environment
(notation
qenv
in
Local/Semantics.v
) any finitely supported function that
maps a pair of participants into a finite sequence (
queue
) of
pairs of labels and sorts.

We define the operations of enqueuing and
dequeuing on queue environments:

\small\begin{array}[]{lll}\texttt{enq}\ Q\ ({\sf p},{\sf q})\ (\ell,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})&=&Q[({\sf p},{\sf q})\mapsfrom Q({\sf p},{\sf q})@(\ell,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})]\\
\texttt{deq}\ Q\ ({\sf p},{\sf q})&=&\texttt{if}\ Q({\sf p},{\sf q})=(\ell,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})\#s\\
&&\texttt{then}\ ((\ell,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}),Q[({\sf p},{\sf q})\mapsfrom s])\ \ \ \texttt{else}\ \texttt{None}\end{array}

We use
\#
as the “cons” constructor for lists and

@
as the “append” operation;
f[x\mapsfrom y]
denotes the
updating of a function
f
in
x
with
y
, namely

f[x\mapsfrom y]\ x^{\prime}=f\ x^{\prime}
for all
x\neq x^{\prime}
and

f[x\mapsfrom y]\ x=y
. We use option types for
partial functions, with
None
as the standard returned
value where the function is undefined.
In case the sequence
Q({\sf p},{\sf q})
is empty
deq
will
not perform any operation on it, but return
None
; in case the
sequence is not empty it will return both its head and its tail (as a
pair). We denote the empty queue environment by
\epsilon
, namely

\epsilon\ ({\sf p},{\sf q})=\texttt{None}
for all
({\sf p},{\sf q})
.

Global trees can represent stages of the execution, where
a participant has already sent a message, but it has not yet been received.
We adapt the “queue projection” from
(
Deniélou and
Yoshida 2013
, Appendix
A.1)
to our coinductive setting, to associate
global trees to the queue contents of a system.

Definition 3.8 (Queue projection).

(Definition
qProject
in

Projection/QProject.v
)

Projection on queue environments
of a global tree
(
queue projection
for short) is the relation

{\small{\_}{\upharpoonright^{\texttt{q}}}{\_}\ :\ \texttt{rel}\ \ {\texttt{g\_ty}^{\texttt{c}}}\ \ {\texttt{qenv}}}
coinductively specified by:

See Appendix
A.4
for more details.

Analogously to queue environments,
we consider all the local
types of the protocol at once.

Definition 3.9 (Local environments).

We call
local environment
, or simply
environment
, any
finitely supported function
\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
that maps participants into
local types.

We are interested in those environments that are defined on the participants
of a global protocol
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and that map each
participant
{\sf p}
to the projection of
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
onto such
{\sf p}
.

Definition 3.10 (Environment projection).

(Definition

eProject
in
Projection/CProject.v
.)

We say that
\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is an environment projection for
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
,
notation
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\upharpoonright\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, if it holds that

\forall{\sf p}.\ \color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ {\upharpoonright^{\texttt{c}}}{{\sf p}}\ (\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E\ {\sf p}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})
.

We define the semantics on
a set of local types together with queue environments. We therefore consider the
projection of a global tree both on local environments and on queue
environments, together in one shot.

Definition 3.11 (One-shot projection).

(Definition

Projection
in
Projection.v
)

We say that the pair of a local environment and of a queue environment
(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)
is a (one-shot) projection for the global tree
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, notation
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\upharpoonright\!\upharpoonright(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)
if it holds that:

\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\upharpoonright\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\quad\text{and}\quad{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\upharpoonright^{\texttt{q}}}{Q}
.

.
Participant
{\sf p}
has sent a message to
{\sf q}
,
{\sf q}
will receive it
next (but has not yet) and then the protocol continues indefinitely
with
{\sf q}
sending a message to
{\sf p}
after the other. We define

\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
such that:

\ \ \color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E\ {\sf p}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{?^{\sf c}[{{\sf q}}];}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{\ell(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}).}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{?^{\sf c}[{{\sf q}}];}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{\ell(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}).\dots}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ \
and

\ \ \color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E\ {\sf q}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{?^{\sf c}[{{\sf p}}];}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{\ell(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}).}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{!^{\sf c}[{{\sf p}}];}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{\ell(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}).}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\allowbreak\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{!^{\sf c}[{{\sf p}}];}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{\ell(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}).\dots}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\
. We then
define
Q

such that:
\ \ Q({\sf p},{\sf q})=[(\ell,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})]\ \
and

\ \ Q({\sf q},{\sf p})=\texttt{None}
. It is easy to verify
that
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\upharpoonright\!\upharpoonright(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)
; observe
that the only “message” enqueued in
Q
is
(\ell,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})
, since
this is the only one sent, but not yet received (at this stage of
the execution).

3.4.
Labelled Transition Relations for Tree Types

At the core of the
trace semantics
for session types
lies a labelled transition system (LTS) defined on trees,
with regard to
actions
. The basic
actions

(datatype
act
in
Common/Actions.v
) of
our asynchronous communication are objects, ranged over by
a
, of the
shape either:

!{\sf p}{\sf q}(\ell,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})
: send
!
action, from participant
{\sf p}
to
participant
{\sf q}
, of label
\ell
and payload type
S
, or

?{\sf q}{\sf p}(\ell,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})
: receive
?
action, from participant
{\sf p}
at
participant
{\sf q}
, of label
\ell
and payload type
S
.
We define the
subject
of an action
a
(definition

subject
in
Common/Actions.v
),
\texttt{subj}\ {a}
, as
{\sf p}
if

a=!{\sf p}{\sf q}(\ell,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})
and as
{\sf q}
if
a=?{\sf q}{\sf p}(\ell,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})
.
3
3

3

The representation of actions is directly taken from

(
Deniélou and
Yoshida 2013
)
, however we have swapped the order of

{\sf p}
and
{\sf q}
in the receive action, so that the subject of an
action always occurs in first position.

Given an action, our types (represented as trees) can perform a
reduction step.

Definition 3.13 (LTS for global trees).

(
step
in
Global/Semantics.v
)
The
labelled transition relation for global trees

(
global reduction
or
global step
for short) is, for each action
a
, the relation

\_\ \xrightarrow{a}\ \_\ :\ \texttt{rel}\ \ {\texttt{g\_ty}^{\texttt{c}}}\ \ {\texttt{g\_ty}^{\texttt{c}}}
inductively specified
by the following clauses:

\small\begin{array}[]{c}\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5469}{\DefTirNameOld{\footnotesize[{g-step-send}]}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\dfrac{a=!{\sf p}{\sf q}(\ell_{j},\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}{}_{j})}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf p}\to{\sf q}:\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xrightarrow{a}\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf p}\mathrel{\hbox to10.55pt{\vbox to12.98pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.27615pt\lower-7.88745pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.93867pt}{-1.225pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{{{{}{}{{}}

The step relation describes a labelled transition system for global
trees with the following intuition:
\DefTirNameOld
[g-step-send]

sending base case
:
with the sending action
!{\sf p}{\sf q}(\ell_{j},\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}{}_{j})
,
a message with label
\ell_{j}
and payload type

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}{}_{j}
is sent by
{\sf p}
, but not yet received by
{\sf q}
;

\DefTirNameOld
[g-step-recv]

receiving base case
: with the receiving
action
?{\sf q}{\sf p}(\ell_{j},\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}{}_{j})
,
a message with label
\ell_{j}
and payload type
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}{}_{j}
,
previously sent by
{\sf p}
, is now received by
{\sf q}
;
in
\DefTirNameOld
[g-step-str1]
, a step is allowed
to be performed under a sending constructor

\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf p}\to{\sf q}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
: each time that the subject of that
action is different from
{\sf p}
and from
{\sf q}
and each
continuation steps;

\DefTirNameOld
[g-step-str2]
with an action
a
a step is allowed
to be performed under a receiving constructor:
each time that the subject of
that action is different from
{\sf q}
(
{\sf p}
has already sent the
message and the label
\ell_{j}
has already been selected), the
continuation corresponding to
\ell_{j}
steps
and others stay as the same.

This semantics allows for some degree of non-determinism. For instance,

\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf p}\to{\sf q}:\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
could perform a step according to both
rules
\DefTirNameOld
[g-step-send]
and
\DefTirNameOld
[g-step-str1]
(depending
on the subject of the action).

Below we define a transition system for environments of local trees, together
with environments of queues.

Definition 3.14 (LTS for
environments).

(
l_step
in

Local/Semantics.v
) The
labelled transition relation for environments

(
local reduction
or
local step
for short) is, for each

a
, the relation

\_\ \xrightarrow{a}\ \_\ :\ \texttt{rel}\ \ {(\texttt{renv}*\texttt{qenv})}\ \ {(\texttt{renv}*\texttt{qenv})}

inductively specified by the following clauses:

\small\begin{array}[]{c}\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5469}{\DefTirNameOld{\footnotesize[{l-step-send}]}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ \dfrac{a=!{\sf p}{\sf q}(\ell_{j},\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}{}_{j})\hskip 9.24994pt\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E\ {\sf p}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{!^{\sf c}[{{\sf q}}];\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{{\sf L^{c}}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)\xrightarrow{a}(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E[{\sf p}\mapsfrom\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{{\sf L^{c}}}{}_{j}]}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\texttt{enq}\ Q\ ({\sf p},{\sf q})\ (\ell_{j},\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}{}_{j}))}\\[14.22636pt]
\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5469}{\DefTirNameOld{\footnotesize[{l-step-recv}]}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ \dfrac{\begin{array}[]{l}a=?{\sf q}{\sf p}(\ell_{j},\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}{}_{j})\\
\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E\ {\sf q}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{?^{\sf c}[{{\sf p}}];\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{{\sf L^{c}}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hskip 9.24994ptQ({\sf p},{\sf q})=(\ell_{j},\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}{}_{j})\#s\end{array}}{(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)\xrightarrow{a}(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E[{\sf q}\mapsfrom\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{{\sf L^{c}}}{}_{j}]}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q[({\sf p},{\sf q})\mapsfrom s])}\end{array}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf p}\to{\sf q}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{(\ell,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}).}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf p}\mathrel{\hbox to7.94pt{\vbox to11.05pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.97081pt\lower-4.73746pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.63333pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

Example 3.15 (Basic steps for global and local trees).

Figure
4(a)
shows the transitions for a global tree,
regulating the sending of a message from
{\sf p}
to
{\sf q}
, and
the local transition for its projection on
{\sf p}
. The
asynchronicity of our system is witnessed by the two different
steps:
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{(g.1)}
, for the sending action

!{\sf p}{\sf q}(\ell,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})
, and
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{(g.2)}
, for the receiving one

?{\sf q}{\sf p}(\ell,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})
. Projecting

\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf p}\to{\sf q}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{(\ell,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}).}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
on
{\sf p}
(arrow

\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{(g.1)}
) gives us a local tree that performs a sending
step
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{(l.1)}
corresponding to
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{(g.1)}
, and
projection is preserved (arrow
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{(p.2)}
). However this
does not happen for the receiving step
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{(g.2)}
: here
the projections on
{\sf p}
of

along

\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{(p.2)}
and of
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
along
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{(p.3)}
are
the same. Dually if we consider the projection on the
receiving participant
{\sf q}
, Figure
4(b)
. Here the
projections along
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{(q.1)}
and
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{(q.2)}

corresponding to the global tree performing a sending action, result
in the same local tree. We have instead a local step

\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{(l.2)}
preserving the local projections on
{\sf q}
along

\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{(q.2)}
and
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{(q.3)}
for the receiving
action along
\color[rgb]{1,0,0}{(g.2)}
.

Figure
4
confirms our intuition: when the global tree
performs one step,
there is one local tree
(namely, one projection of the
global tree) such that it performs a corresponding
step. We have indeed defined semantics for collections of local trees,
as opposed to single local trees. The formal relation of the
small-step reductions with respect to projection is established with
soundness and completeness results (see
Appendix
A

for proof outlines).

Theorem 3.16 (Step Soundness).

(Theorem
Project_step
in
TraceEquiv.v
) If
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xrightarrow{a}\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\upharpoonright\!\upharpoonright(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)
,
there exist
\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and
Q^{\prime}
such that

\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\upharpoonright\!\upharpoonright(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime})
and

(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)\xrightarrow{a}(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime})
.

Theorem 3.17 (Step Completeness).

(Theorem

Project_lstep
in
TraceEquiv.v
) If
(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)\xrightarrow{a}(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime})
and

\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\upharpoonright\!\upharpoonright(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)
, there exist
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
such that

\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\upharpoonright\!\upharpoonright(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime})
and

\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xrightarrow{a}\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
.

3.5.
Trace Semantics and Trace Equivalence

We finally show trace equivalence for global and
local types with
our Coq development of semantics for coinductive trees.

Definition 3.18 (Traces).

(Codatatype
trace
in
Action.v
), ranged
over by
t
, are terms generated
coinductively
by

\ \ t\ \ \ ::=\ []\;\mathbin{\mathbf{|\!\!|}}\;{a}\#{t}\ \
where
a

is any action, as defined in
§
3.4
4
4

4

For traces, we use the same notation
as for lists, however we bear in mind that this definiton is
coinductive: it generates possibly infinite streams.
.

We associate traces to the execution of global trees and local environments.

Definition 3.19 (Admissible traces for a global
tree).

We say that a trace is admissible for a global tree if the
coinductive relation
\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{g}}\;{\_}\;{\_}
(definition
g_lts
in

Global/Semantics.v
) holds:

\dfrac{}{\phantom{\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{g}}\;{[]}\;{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\mathtt{end}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}}

\dfrac{\phantom{}}{\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{g}}\;{[]}\;{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\mathtt{end}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}

\dfrac{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xrightarrow{a}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\hskip 9.24994pt\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{g}}\;{t}\;{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}{\phantom{\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{g}}\;{{a}\#{t}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}}

\dfrac{\phantom{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xrightarrow{a}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\hskip 9.24994pt\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{g}}\;{t}\;{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}}{\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{g}}\;{{a}\#{t}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}

Definition 3.20 (Admissible traces for environments).

We say that a trace is admissible for a pair of a local environment
and a queue environment if the coinductive relation
\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{l}}\;{\_}\;{\_}

(definition
l_lts
in
Local/Semantics.v
) holds:

\dfrac{\forall{\sf p}.\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ {\sf p}=\texttt{None}}{\phantom{\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{l}}\;{[]}\;{(}\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\epsilon)}}

\dfrac{\phantom{\forall{\sf p}.\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ {\sf p}=\texttt{None}}}{\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{l}}\;{[]}\;{(}\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\epsilon)}

\dfrac{(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)\xrightarrow{a}(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime})\hskip 9.24994pt\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{l}}\;{t}\;{(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime})}}{\phantom{\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{l}}\;{{a}\#{t}}\;{(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)}}}

\dfrac{\phantom{(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)\xrightarrow{a}(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime})\hskip 9.24994pt\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{l}}\;{t}\;{(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime})}}}{\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{l}}\;{{a}\#{t}}\;{(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)}}

Observe that generally more than one execution trace
are admissible for a global tree or for an environment
5
5

5

About non-determinism in our semantics, see
Remark
A.30
, Appendix
A
.
.

We can now state the
trace equivalence
theorem, our final result
for multiparty session types. We sketch an outline of the proof in Appendix
A
, Theorem
A.38
.

Theorem 3.21 (Trace equivalence).

(Theorem

TraceEquivalence
in
TraceEquiv.v
.)

If
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\upharpoonright\!\upharpoonright(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)
, then

\ \texttt{tr}^{\texttt{g}}\;{t}\;{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\

if and only if
\ \texttt{tr}^{\texttt{l}}\;{t}\;{(}\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)\
.

Trace equivalence for global and local types (trees)
concludes our formalisation of the metatheory
of multiparty session types: squares

(M.1)
and
(M.2)
of
the diagram
in Figure

2
. In the next section we specify a language
for communicating systems inside Coq and extend extend the trace equivalence
result to well-typed processes.

4.
A Certified Process Language

This section defines
Zooid
, an embedded domain specific language
in Coq for specifying certified multiparty processes.
Zooid

combines shallow and deep embedding: on one hand process actions are
deeply embedded, represented as an inductive type; on the other, the
exchanged values, and computations applied to them are a shallow
embedding expressed as Gallina terms. The core process calculus of

Zooid
is session-typed, where the typing derivation is described
as a Coq inductive predicate. The constructs of
Zooid
are

smart constructors
that build both a process, and a proof that
this is well-typed with respect to a given local type. Each
process is single threaded and the concurrent semantics occurs due
to the asynchronous nature of the channels.

4.1.
Core Processes

\begin{array}[]{l}\hskip-14.22636pt\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5469}{\DefTirNameOld{\footnotesize[{p-ty-end}]}}\\
\dfrac{}{\Gamma\vdash_{\text{{lt}}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{finish}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}:\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\end{array}\ \begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5469}{\DefTirNameOld{\footnotesize[{p-ty-jump}]}}\\

\begin{array}[]{cc}\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5469}{\DefTirNameOld{\footnotesize[{p-ty-write}]}}\\
\dfrac{\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\texttt{act}_{w}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}:\llbracket\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rrbracket\to\texttt{unit}\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{v}}:\llbracket\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rrbracket\hskip 9.24994pt\Gamma\vdash_{\text{{lt}}}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}:\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\Gamma\vdash_{\text{{lt}}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{write}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\texttt{act}_{w}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{v}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}:\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\end{array}\\

\end{array}

Figure 5
.
Process Typing System

The core process calculus of
Zooid
differs to those generally
used in the session-types literature in several aspects. First, the
combination of shallow and deep embedding implies that a process may
be defined in terms of a larger expression of the ambient calculus.
Secondly, the process calculus does not include parallel composition.
Just as
“zooid”
, in biology, is used to refer to the single
individual in a colonial organism, a process
proc
implements the
behaviour of a single participant in the distributed system:
we are interested in certifying processes in
isolation to the larger system. This approach plays well with the usual MPST
methodology and it admits heterogenous development, as in one can use

Zooid
for the critical roles and other roles can be implemented in
different languages, using different frameworks.

Definition 4.1 (Syntax of untyped processes).

Processes,
proc
(definition
Proc
in
Proc.v
), are
embedded in an ambient calculus
\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
. In our implementation,

proc
is the inductive type of processes, of type
Proc
, and the
ambient calculus is Gallina, the specification language of Coq.

\begin{array}[]{rl}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Coloneqq&\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{proc}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}+\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{\texttt{if}\;\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{\texttt{then}\;\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{\texttt{else}\;\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}}\\
\mid&\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{\texttt{fun}\;\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{x}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid\ldots\hskip 14.22636pt\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{n}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 14.22636pt\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\ell_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\in\mathbb{N}\\
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{proc}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\in\texttt{Proc}\Coloneqq&\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{finish}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{jump}\;\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{loop}\;\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\;\{\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\}}\\
\mid&\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{recv}\;{\sf p}\;\{\ell_{i}.\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{i}\}}{}_{i\in I}\mid\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{send}\;{\sf p}\;(\ell,\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}).\;\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}}\\
\mid&\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{read}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\texttt{act}_{r}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}}\;(x.{\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}})}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{write}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\texttt{act}_{w}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{v}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}}}\\
\mid&\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{interact}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\texttt{act}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{v}}\;(x.{\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}})}\end{array}

The constructs of
Proc
mirror those of local types:
finish
is the

ended
process;
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{jump}\;\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is a
jump

to recursion variable
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
;
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{loop}\;\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\;\{\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is a

recursive process
, built by expression
\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, that
introduces a new recursion variable
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
;

\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{recv}\;{\sf p}\;\{\ell_{i}.\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{i}\}}{}_{i\in I}
is the process

receiving
from
{\sf p}
a message with label
\ell_{i}
, a
value
x
, and continues as
(\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{i}\;x)
; and

\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{send}\;{\sf p}\;(\ell,\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{1}).\;\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is the
sending

process with label
\ell
and expression
\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{1}
to participant

{\sf p}
, and then continues as
\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{2}
. Our calculus does not include
parallel composition: we assume that the system is implemented as the
parallel composition of all the participants. For example, the
following is a process that receives requests from a participant
{\sf p}

and replies increasing the received number by
m
, until
{\sf p}
chooses
to finish:

\small\begin{array}[]{ll}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{proc}}{}_{\sf q}=&\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{loop}\;\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\{}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{recv}\;{\sf p}\{}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\ell_{1}.}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{\texttt{fun}\;\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{x}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{send}\;{\sf p}\;(\ell_{1},\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{x+m}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}).\;}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}}\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{jump}\;\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{;}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\ell_{2}.\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{\texttt{fun}\;\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{x}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}\Rightarrow\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{finish}}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\}}\end{array}

A process can be defined mixing Gallina terms and
proc
. For example, in the
process above, the term
\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{x+m}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is a term in Gallina. These Gallina
terms can be used to specify branching in the control flow of the process.
The process below is one possible implementation for
{\sf p}
that loops
until the value received is greater than some threshold
n
:

\small\begin{array}[]{rcl}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{\sf p}&=&\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{\texttt{fun}\;x\Rightarrow}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{\texttt{if}\;x>n}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{\texttt{then}\;\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{send}\;{\sf q}\;(\ell_{2},\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{\texttt{tt}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}).\;\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{finish}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}}\\
&&\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{\texttt{else}\;\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{send}\;{\sf q}\;(\ell_{1},\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{x}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}).\;\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{jump}\;\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}}\par\\
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{proc}}{}_{\sf p}&=&\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{send}\;{\sf q}\;(\ell_{1},\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}).\;\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{loop}\;\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\;\{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{recv}\;{\sf q}\;\{\ell_{1}.\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{\sf p}\}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}}\end{array}

Zooid
processes interact with their environment by calling
functions written in the language of the runtime (OCaml in this case).
These functions exchange information between
Zooid
and the
environment in a safe way by not exposing channels or the transport
API. The interaction happens by calling an external function:
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\texttt{act}_{r}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
,

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\texttt{act}_{w}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, and
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\texttt{act}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
for reading, writing or interacting with the
environment.
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\texttt{act}_{r}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is a function that takes a unit and returns a
value of payload type (i.e.: a
coq_ty T
for some type

T
).
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\texttt{act}_{w}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is a function that takes a parameter of payload
type and returns unit, allowing the process to call OCaml to print on
the screen or write to file or similar things. Finally
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\texttt{act}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is the
action function that passes data to the OCaml runtime and receives
some response, thus combining the two other environment interaction
functions. These functions
do not affect the communication structure

of the process: they are internal actions and do not appear
in the trace of the process.

Definition 4.2 (Process typing system).

We define typing for processes

\Gamma\vdash_{\text{{lt}}}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}:\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
in Figure
5
, as an inductive predicate in Coq
(definition
of_lt
in
Proc.v
). Since
proc
is
embedded in Coq, we assume the standard typing judgement for Gallina
terms, of the form
\Gamma\vdash\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}:T
. We assume a set of
sorts
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}{}_{j}
, and an encoding as a Coq type

\llbracket\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}{}_{j}\rrbracket
(see Definition
3.1
).

Rules
\DefTirNameOld
[p-ty-end]
,
\DefTirNameOld
[p-ty-jump]
, and

\DefTirNameOld
[p-ty-loop]
state that the local type of the ended process, a
jump to
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, and recursion are
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
,
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, and a recursive
type respectively. Rule
\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5469}{\DefTirNameOld{\footnotesize[{p-ty-send}]}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
specifies that a
send process with label
\ell
has a send type, if
\ell
is in
the set of accepted labels. Rule

\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5469}{\DefTirNameOld{\footnotesize[{p-ty-recv}]}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
specifies that a receive process has a receive type, if all the alternatives
have the correct local type for all possible payloads

x:\llbracket\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}{}_{i}\rrbracket
. Any expression
\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
that does
not match any of these rules must be proven to be of the correct
type for all of its possible reductions. For example, it is
straightforward to prove that if
\Gamma\vdash_{\text{{lt}}}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{t}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}:\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

and
\Gamma\vdash_{\text{{lt}}}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{f}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}:\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
then

\Gamma\vdash_{\text{{lt}}}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{\texttt{if}\;\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{\texttt{then}\;\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{t}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{\texttt{else}\;\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{f}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}:\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

by case analysis on
\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
. Finally, rules
\DefTirNameOld
[p-ty-read]
,

\DefTirNameOld
[p-ty-write]
, and
\DefTirNameOld
[p-ty-interact]
, have no impact on
the local type, so they simply check that the actions are well typed,
and that the continuation process has the expected type.

4.2.
Zooid

In the Coq library
Zooid.v
,
Zooid
terms (ranged over by

Z
) are dependent pairs of a
proc
, and a proof that it is
well-typed with respect to a given local type
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
.

⬇

Definition

wt_proc

L

:=

{

P

:

Proc

|

of_lt

P

L

}.

They are built using smart constructors, helper functions and
notations to define processes that are well-typed by construction
(i.e.: a process and a witness of its type derivation). Moreover, we
take care that the local type of each smart constructor is fully
determined by their inputs, so we can use Coq to
infer
the
local type of each of these processes. Given a
Zooid
expression

Z
, we can project the
first component
to extract the
underlying
proc
term. Since the behaviour of alternatives in

Z
terms is fully specified, we can infer its local type. By
construction, if a term

can be defined, then its underlying

proc
is well-typed with respect to some local type
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
,

second component
of the dependent pair.

The simplest example is the

l_end
. Coq infers most parameters.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{Z}}{}^{b}}&\Coloneqq&\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\;\ell,\;x:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\texttt{?}\;}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{Z}}\\

&&\mid\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{send}\;{\sf p}\;(\ell,\;\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0})\texttt{!}\;}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{Z}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mid\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{recv}\;{\sf p}\;(\ell,\;\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{x}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0})\texttt{?}\;}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{Z}}\\

&&\mid\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{select}\;{\sf p}\;\;}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}[\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{Z}}{}^{s}}{}_{1}\mid\ldots\mid\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{Z}}{}^{s}}{}_{n}]\end{array}}\\
