Figure 6
.
Zooid
Syntax

The syntactic constructs are the expected, with only a few
differences:

(a)

if
then
else
is a
Zooid
construct since it needs to carry the
proof that the underlying proc is well-typed;

(b)

branch and select must take a list of alternatives (
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{Z}}{}^{b}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{Z}}{}^{s}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
respectively),
and send/receive are defined as branch/select with a singleton alternative.

The alternatives for branch,
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{Z}}{}^{b}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, are pairs of labels and
continuations. The alternatives for
select
,
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{Z}}{}^{s}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
are:

(1)

\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{case}\;\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{1}\Rightarrow\ell,\;\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{2}:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\texttt{!}\;}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{Z}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, specifies
to send
\ell
and
\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{2}:\llbracket\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rrbracket

and then continue as
Z
,
when

\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{1}
evaluates to
true
;

(2)

\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{otherwise}\Rightarrow\ell,\;\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{2}:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\texttt{!}\;}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{Z}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, specifies that the default alternative
is to send
\ell
and
\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{2}
, and then continue as
Z
; and

(3)

\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{skip}\Rightarrow\ell,\;\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\texttt{!}\;}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, specifies the unimplemented alternative of sending

\ell
and a value of sort
S
, and then continuing as
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
.

We require
skip
to enforce a unique local type: since
Definition
4.2
does not include subtyping,

Zooid
requires that all the possible behaviours in the local type
must be either implemented or declared. We impose a syntactic
condition on
select
: there must be exactly one default
case, which must occur after the last
case
. The three
constructs to interact with external code (
read
,

write
, and
interact
) are similar to
their untyped counterparts from §
4.1
. These actions do not impact the traces nor the local types, so
they simply sport the local type of their continuations.

4.3.
Semantics of
Zooid

The semantics of
Zooid
is defined as a labelled transition system of the
underlying
proc
terms, analogously to that of local type
trees in Definition
3.14
6
6

6

For the sake of uniformity, here we present
the LTS for processes as a relation,
however in Coq we define it, equivalently,
as a recursive function:
do_step_proc
in
Proc.v
.

, but with values instead of sorts in the trace,
and explicitly unfolding recursion.

Definition 4.4 (LTS for processes).

The
LTS for processes

is, for each action
a
, defined as:

\begin{array}[]{c}\begin{array}[]{lll}\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5469}{\DefTirNameOld{\footnotesize[{p-step-send}]}}&\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5469}{\DefTirNameOld{\footnotesize[{p-step-recv}]}}\\
\dfrac{a=!{\sf p}{\sf q}(\ell,\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{1})\hskip 9.24994pt}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{send}\;{\sf q}\;(\ell,\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{1}).\;\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xrightarrow{a}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{2}}&\dfrac{a=?{\sf q}{\sf p}(\ell_{\bar{\imath}},\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})\hskip 9.24994pt}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{recv}\;{\sf p}\;\{\ell_{i}.\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{i}\}}{}_{i\in I}\xrightarrow{a}(\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{\bar{\imath}}\;\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}&\end{array}\\[14.22636pt]
\begin{array}[]{l}\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5469}{\DefTirNameOld{\footnotesize[{p-step-loop}]}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\dfrac{[(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{loop}\;\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\;\{\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})/(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{jump}\;\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})]\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xrightarrow{a}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}^{\prime}}{(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{loop}\;\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\;\{\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})\xrightarrow{a}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}^{\prime}}\end{array}\end{array}

The steps of the LTS are:
\DefTirNameOld
[p-step-send]
states that a send
process transitions to the continuation
\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{2}
with the action that sends a
label
\ell
and value
\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{1}
;
\DefTirNameOld
[p-step-recv]
states that a receive
process transitions to
(\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}_{\bar{\imath}}\;\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})
with the receive action
from participant
{\sf p}
; and
\DefTirNameOld
[p-step-loop]
unfolds recursion once to
perform a step on a recursive process.

We prove the type preservation for
\vdash_{\text{{lt}}}
. To show this, we need to
relate process actions with local/global type actions. This is done by a simple
erasure that removes the values, but preserves the types in an action, denoted
by
|a|
. For example, if
a=!{\sf p}{\sf q}(\ell,\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})
and
\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}:\llbracket\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rrbracket
, then
|a|=!{\sf p}{\sf q}(\ell,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})
.

Theorem 4.5 (Type preservation).

(Theorem
preservation
in the file
Proc.v
.)
If
\Gamma\vdash_{\text{{lt}}}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}:\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and
\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xrightarrow{a}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}^{\prime}
, then there exists

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}{}^{\prime}
such that
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xrightarrow{|a|}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}{}^{\prime}
, and
\Gamma\vdash_{\text{{lt}}}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}{}^{\prime}:\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}{}^{\prime}
.

We write
\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{p}}\;{t}\;{\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
to express that a trace
t
is admissible
by process
\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
. The formal definition goes analogously to Definition

3.20
for
\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{l}}\;{\_}\;{\_}
; note, however, that the admission of a trace by
process is checked
in isolation
to other processes.
To relate process traces to global/local type traces we need to define the
notion of a
complete subtrace
.

Definition 4.6 (Complete subtrace).

We say that
t_{1}
is a complete subtrace of
t_{2}
for participant
{\sf p}
(definition
subtrace
in
Local.v
), if all
actions in
t_{2}
that have
{\sf p}
as a subject occur in
t_{1}
in the same
relative position (i.e. the
n
-th action of
{\sf p}
in
t_{2}
must be the
n
-th action
of
t_{1}
). We write
t_{1}\preceq_{\sf p}t_{2}
as the greatest relation
satisfying:

\dfrac{\texttt{subj}\ {a}\neq{\sf p}\hskip 9.24994ptt_{1}\preceq_{\sf p}t_{2}}{\phantom{t_{1}\preceq_{\sf p}({a}\#{t}_{2})}}

\dfrac{\phantom{\texttt{subj}\ {a}\neq{\sf p}\hskip 9.24994ptt_{1}\preceq_{\sf p}t_{2}}}{t_{1}\preceq_{\sf p}({a}\#{t}_{2})}

\dfrac{\texttt{subj}\ {a}={\sf p}\hskip 9.24994ptt_{1}\preceq_{\sf p}t_{2}}{\phantom{({a}\#{t}_{1})\preceq_{\sf p}({a}\#{t}_{2})}}

\dfrac{\phantom{\texttt{subj}\ {a}={\sf p}\hskip 9.24994ptt_{1}\preceq_{\sf p}t_{2}}}{({a}\#{t}_{1})\preceq_{\sf p}({a}\#{t}_{2})}

\dfrac{}{\phantom{[]\preceq_{\sf p}[]}}

\dfrac{\phantom{}}{[]\preceq_{\sf p}[]}

The main result for
Zooid
states that for all admissible traces
for a well-typed process, there exists at least a trace in the larger
system that is a complete supertrace of that of the process. We state
this formally as Theorem
4.7

(
process_traces_are_global_types
in

Proc.v
). Thus, well-typed processes inherit the global type properties of
protocol compliance, deadlock freedom and liveness.

Theorem 4.7 (Process and global type traces).

Let
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\upharpoonright\!\upharpoonright(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\epsilon)
and
\Gamma\vdash_{\text{{lt}}}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}:\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

such that
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\Re\;(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;{\sf p})
. Then, for all traces
t_{\sf p}
such
that
\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{p}}\;{t_{\sf p}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{e}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
there exists a trace
t
such that

\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{g}}\;{t}\;{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, and
|t_{\sf p}|\preceq_{\sf p}t
.

t=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{a_{1}}\#{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{a_{2}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}\#{{a_{3}}\#{\ldots}}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

in

4.4.
Extraction

Terms of type
Proc
, in Coq, can be easily extracted to executable OCaml code,
following an approach similar to that of Interaction Trees
(
Xia et al. 2019
)
:
we can substitute the occurrences of
proc
terms by a suitable OCaml handler.
Figure
8
shows the declaration of a module for that purpose.

⬇

Module

ProcessMonad
.

Parameter

t

:

Type

\rightarrow\;

Type
.

(*

monadic

bind

and

pure

values

*)

Parameter

:

forall

T1

T2
,

t

\rightarrow\;

(

\rightarrow\;

t

T2
)

\rightarrow\;

t

T2
.

:

T1
,

T1

\rightarrow\;

T1
.

(*

actions

to

send

and

*)

forall

T
,

\rightarrow\;

T

\rightarrow\;

.

(

\rightarrow\;

)

.

ProcessMonad

Figure 8
.
The Process Monad.

Module
ProcessMonad
specifies a monadic type
t
, that supports the
standard
bind
and
pure
operations, as well as constructs
for adding the required effects, in this case network communication
and looping (with potential non-termination). During extraction this
module becomes the ambient monad for the extracted code. In order to
run the code the user instantiates the monad
to provide a low
level implementation, which fills in the details about the network
transport.
Zooid

processes are translated into the monad using the function

extract_proc
from
Proc.v
.
Appendix
B

shows the function in its entirety.

4.5.
Runtime

The code for an endpoint process is extracted as a value inside of the
process monad from §
4.4
.
Zooid
’s runtime
provides an implementation of
ProcessMonad
. The endpoint process is independent of the transport and network
protocols; the exact specification of those is deferred to the
implementation of the monad.
The runtime implements the monad relying on
the monad provided by OCaml’s Lwt
library
7
7

7

https://ocsigen.org/lwt/5.2.0/manual/manual
,
as well as its asynchronous communication primitives.
The transport uses TCP/IP and the payloads are encoded and decoded
using the ’Marshal’ module in OCaml’s standard
library
8
8

8

https://ocaml.org/releases/4.11/htmlman/libref/Marshal.html
.
This design prioritises OCaml based technologies to implement
asynchronous I/O and data encoding. Other transports are possible (e.g., web services over HTTP).

4.5.1.
Implementation

In
Zooid
, the user implements their processes in the DSL, then uses Coq to produce OCaml code for the monad’s module type and for the process, using extraction. The runtime implements a means to run that
code. Concretely it provides the transport and serialization.

A runnable process amounts to an instance of the functor type in
Figure
9
, in which we provide the process monad
instance together with the extracted process.

⬇

module

type

PROCESS_FUNCTOR

=

functor

(
MP

:

ProcessMonad
)

->

sig

module

PM

:

sig

type

’
x

t

=

’
x

MP
.
t

val

run

:

’
a1

t

->

’
a1

val

send

:

role

->

lbl

->

’
a1

->

unit

t

val

recv

:

role

->

(
lbl

->

unit

t
)

->

unit

t

val

recv_one

:

role

->

’
a1

t

val

bind

:

’
a1

t

->

(’
a1

->

’
a2

t
)

->

’
a2

t

val

pure

:

’
a1

->

’
a1

t

val

loop

:

var

->

(
unit

->

’
a1

t
)

->

’
a1

t

val

set_current

:

var

->

unit

t

end

val

proc

:

unit

MP
.
t

end

Figure 9
.
The Process Functor.

Communication primitives in processes are unaware of transport or
other networking issues, they simply expect to be able to communicate
with the other roles involved in the protocol. The runtime
implementation requires the user to provide for each role a list of
channels to communicate with the other roles. It is specified as:

⬇

type

connection_spec

=

Server

of

sockaddr

|

Client

of

sockaddr

type

conn_desc

=

{

role_to

:

role
;

spec

:

connection_spec

}

where each process needs to specify a
conn_desc

list

detailing a channel to each role where it either starts a connection
(using the
Client
connector and specifiying IP and port in
the
sockaddr
datatype) or waits for a connection (in a
similar way using the
Server
constructor).

So finally, the runtime is invoked by calling the function:

⬇

val

execute_extracted_process

:

conn_desc

list

->

(
module

PROCESS_FUNCTOR
)

->

unit

which connects a participant to all the roles as specified in the connection
list and executes extracted process passed as first-class module value
to the function. If the extracted process interacts with OCaml code, the library that
implements all the external functions has to be compiled into the
executable.

With the addition of the runtime
Zooid
processes become certified
code that can be readily executed to implement distributed multiparty
services.

5.
Evaluation: Certified Processes

This section displays several common use cases in the MPST literature,
implemented and certified using
Zooid
: (1) several implementations of a
recursive ping-pong protocol; (2) a recursive pipeline; and (3) the two-buyer
protocol from
(
Honda
et al. 2008
)
. We conclude the section with
a summary evaluating our mechanisation effort.

A Common Workflow

Our workflow consists of the following
steps:

(1)

specify the global type for the protocol;

(2)

project the global type into the set of local types;

(3)

implement a process using
Zooid
;

(4)

(if necessary) prove that the local type of the process
is equal up to unravelling to the projection of some participant;

(5)

use extraction to OCaml; and

(6)

implement external OCaml actions (if any).

Steps
(1)
,
(3)
, and
(6)
are the
necessary inputs for implementing a certified process. Steps

(2)
and
(5)
are fully automated, and step

(4)
is often automated too, although it may require a
simple manual proof. Finally, while step

(5)
is fully automated, it is possible to control the
result by using common Coq commands (e.g. marking some definitions
opaque to avoid inlining them).

5.1.
Examples of Certified Processes

Pipeline.

We start with a recursive variant of the example in
§
2.3
. The first step is to specify the global type.
We write its inductive representation:

\small\begin{array}[]{ll}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.8}{Definition}}\;\texttt{pipeline}:=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}.}&\hskip-5.69054pt\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\sf Alice}}\to{{\sf Bob}}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\\
&\hskip-5.69054pt\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\sf Bob}}\to{{\sf Carol}}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}.\end{array}

The next step is to project
pipeline
into all of its participants.
There are two reasons to apply the projection at this step: (1) only
well-formed protocols are projectable; and (2) we obtain the local
types that will guide the implementation: the local types
will need to typecheck the implemented processes. If the global type
is not projectable, or the processes do not implement the resulting
local types (or one of their unrollings), then we cannot guarantee
anything about a
Zooid
implementation of any participant. We
define a notation for performing the projection of all participants:

\small\texttt{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.8}{Definition}}\;\texttt{pipeline${}_{lt}$}:=\texttt{\textbackslash}\texttt{project}\;\texttt{pipeline}.

If
pipeline
is not well-formed, then
\
project
will not

typecheck. Otherwise,
pipeline
lt
will be a list of pairs of
participants and local types. This list will contain an entry for

{{\sf Alice}}
,
{{\sf Bob}}
and
{{\sf Carol}}
. We get local type for
{{\sf Bob}}
with:

\small\texttt{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.8}{Definition}}\;\texttt{bob${}_{lt}$}:=\texttt{\textbackslash}\texttt{get}\;\textsf{Bob}\;\texttt{pipeline${}_{lt}$}.

The notation
\
get
expands into a lookup in
pipeline
lt
that
requires a proof that
{{\sf Bob}}
is in
pipeline
lt
. If we write

\texttt{\textbackslash}\texttt{get}\;{\sf p}\;\allowbreak\texttt{pipeline${}_{lt}$}
with some
{\sf p}\not\in\texttt{pipeline${}_{lt}$}
, then the
command will fail to typecheck. There are now two possibilities for
using
bob
lt
to implement
{{\sf Bob}}
: (1) providing
bob
lt
as a type
index; or (2) omitting
bob
lt
, inferring the local type, and then
proving that the inferred local type is equal to
bob
lt
up to
unravelling. Here we use (1), but sometimes the process actually
implements an unrolling of the local type. We will show examples of
(2) in the next section.

\small\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.8}{Definition}}\;\texttt{bob}:\texttt{wt\_proc}\;\texttt{bob${}_{lt}$}\\
\hskip 9.24994pt:=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{loop}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\;\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{recv}\;{{\sf Alice}}\;(\ell,\;x:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0})\texttt{?}\;}\\
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{interact}\;\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\texttt{compute}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\ \ x}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ (\texttt{fun}\ res\ \Rightarrow\\
\ \ {\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{send}\;{{\sf Carol}}\;(\ell,\;\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{res}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0})\texttt{!}\;}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{jump}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})).\end{array}\end{array}

With
Zooid
’s

interact
command we can call
the
compute
function, which is implemented in
OCaml, allowing any arbitrary computation safely because the runtime
hides the communication channels to prevent errors.

Finally, to do extraction to OCaml, we call
\texttt{extract\_proc}:\texttt{Proc}\to\texttt{MP\!.\!t}
. The user has options for code extraction: (1)
since
Proc
is defined inductively, use Coq’s
Eval

compute
to first replace all occurrences of
Proc
to

MP​.​t
; (2) extract the inductive representation, as well as

extract_proc
. The former may evaluate and unfold more terms than desired. To
control this, we use Coq’s command
Opaque
to specify any function or
definition that we do not wish to be unfolded.

Ping-Pong.

In the anonymous supplement, we present
several implementations of the clients of a ping-pong server.
The global protocol is:

\small\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.8}{Definition}}\;\texttt{ping\_pong}:=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}.}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\sf Alice}}\to{{\sf Bob}}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ \{\\
\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\ell_{1}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{unit}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\;\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0};\;\ \ell_{2}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\sf Bob}}\to{{\sf Alice}}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell_{3}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\}.\end{array}

Here,
{{\sf Alice}}
acts as the client for
{{\sf Bob}}
, which is the ping-pong server.

{{\sf Alice}}
can send zero or more
ping
messages (label
\ell_{2}
), and
finally quitting (label
\ell_{1}
).
{{\sf Bob}}
, for each ping received, will reply a

pong
message (label
\ell_{3}
). In particular, we
wish to implement a client that sends an undefined number of pings,
stopping when the server replies with a natural number greater than
some
k
. We show below the
Zooid
specification:

\small\hskip 2.84526pt\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.8}{Definition}}\;\texttt{alice}:\texttt{typed\_proc}:=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{[\texttt{proc}}\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{select}\;{{\sf Bob}}\;\hskip-8.53581pt\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\hskip 8.53581pt[\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{skip}\Rightarrow\ell_{1},\;\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{unit}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\texttt{!}\;}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{end}}\\
\mid\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{otherwise}\Rightarrow\ell_{2},\;0:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\texttt{!}\;}\\
\ \ \color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{loop}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\;(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{recv}\;{{\sf Bob}}\;(\ell_{3},\;x:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0})\texttt{?}\;}\\
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{select}\;{{\sf Bob}}\;\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}[\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{case}\;x\geq k\Rightarrow\ell_{1},\;\texttt{tt}:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{unit}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\texttt{!}\;}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{finish}}\\
\mid\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{otherwise}\Rightarrow\ell_{2},\;x:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\texttt{!}\;}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{jump}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}])]]\end{array}}\end{array}}\end{array}\end{array}

We project
ping_pong
and get the expected
local type for
{{\sf Alice}}
:
alice
lt
.
We observe that here the local type for

alice
is not syntactically equal to
alice
lt
:

\small\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{alice${}_{lt}$}=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mu\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}.\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{![{{{\sf Bob}}}];}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\{\ell_{1}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{unit}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0};\ell_{2}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{?[{{{\sf Bob}}}];}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell_{3}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\}\par\\[4.62497pt]
\texttt{projT1}\;\texttt{alice}=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{![{{{\sf Bob}}}];}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\{\ \ell_{1}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{unit}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0};\;\ell_{2}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mu\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}.\\
\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{?[{{{\sf Bob}}}];}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell_{3}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{![{{{\sf Bob}}}];}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\{\ell_{1}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{unit}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0};\;\ell_{2}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}.\}\}\par\end{array}

This is not a problem since a simple proof by coinduction can show
that both types unravel to the same local tree. This gains the flexibility to have processes that implement any unrolling of
their local type, and the proofs are mostly simple as they follow the
way the types were unrolled. See
Appendix
B.1

for
more details on how to
construct gradually this client, showing how to iteratively
program using
Zooid
.

5.2.
A Certified Two Buyer Protocol

We conclude this section presenting an implementation of the

two-buyer protocol

(
Honda
et al. 2008
)
, a common
benchmark of MPST. This is a protocol for an online purchase service
that enables customers to split the cost of an item among two
participants, as long as they agree on their shares. First, buyer

A
queries the seller
S
for an item. Then,

S
sends the item cost first to
A
, then to

B
. Then,

A
sends a proposed share for the item.

B
then either accepts the proposal, and receives the delivery
date from
S
, or rejects the proposal.

\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.8}{Definition}}\;\texttt{two\_buyer}:=\ \color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\texttt{A}\to\texttt{S}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\texttt{ItemId}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\\
\hskip 8.50012pt\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\texttt{S}\to\texttt{A}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\texttt{Quote}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\texttt{S}\to\texttt{B}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\texttt{Quote}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\\
\hskip 8.50012pt\par\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\texttt{A}\to\texttt{B}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\texttt{Propose}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\texttt{B}\to\texttt{S}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\{\ \texttt{Accept}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\\
\hskip 8.50012pt\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\texttt{S}\to\texttt{B}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\texttt{Date}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0};\ \texttt{Reject}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{unit}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\;\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\}\end{array}\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\rule{137.9979pt}{0.4pt}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\texttt{B}_{\texttt{lt}}:=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{?[{\texttt{S}}];}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\texttt{Quote}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{?[{\texttt{A}}];}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\texttt{Propose}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{![{\texttt{S}}];}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\{\texttt{Accept}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\\
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{?[{\texttt{S}}];}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\texttt{Date}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0};\texttt{Reject}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{unit}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\}\end{array}\\
\lx@intercol\hfil\rule{137.9979pt}{0.4pt}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.8}{Definition}}\;\texttt{buyer}_{\texttt{B}}:\texttt{wt\_proc}\;\texttt{B}_{\texttt{lt}}:=\\
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{recv}\;\texttt{S}\;(\texttt{Quote},\;x:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0})\texttt{?}\;}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{recv}\;\texttt{A}\;(\texttt{Propose},\;y:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0})\texttt{?}\;}\\
\hskip 8.50012pt\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{select}\;\texttt{S}\;\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}[\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{case}\;y>=\texttt{divn}\;x\;3\Rightarrow\texttt{Accept},\;y-x:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\texttt{!}\;}\\
\ \ \color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{recv}\;\texttt{S}\;(\texttt{Date},\;d:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0})\texttt{?}\;}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{finish}}\\
\mid\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{otherwise}\Rightarrow\texttt{Reject},\;\texttt{tt}:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{unit}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\texttt{!}\;}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{finish}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}]\end{array}}\end{array}\end{array}

Figure 10
.
The Two Buyer protocol

Figure
10
shows the protocol as a global type,
the local type,
\texttt{B}_{\texttt{lt}}
, that results from the projection on
B
,
and a possible implementation of the role of
B
in
Zooid
.
Different implementations of the local type
will differ in how the choice is made, but the local type
will always need to be syntactically equal to the projected
\texttt{B}_{\texttt{lt}}
,
due to the absence of recursion. In the implementation chosen in
Figure
10
, the participant
B

will reject any proposal where
B
pays
more than one third of the cost of the item.
This implementation is
guaranteed
to behave as
B
in
the protocol
two_buyer
, hence deadlock-free. Our workflow
preserves the ability to define and implement each participant independently:

A
and
S
could be implemented in any language, as long as they
are implemented using a compatible transport to that of the OCaml implementation
of
MP​.​t
. The code that checks the types and performs the
projections is certified, as it is exactly the same code about which the
properties were established.

5.3.
Mechanisation Effort

The development is 7.3KLOC of Coq code, and 1.7KLOC of OCaml
for the runtime (including examples). The certified code consists of
269 definitions, including functions and (co)inductive definitions
and 396 proved lemmas and theorems. The most challenging part was
working out the right definitions: the finite syntax object/infinite
unrolling correspondence felt like a convoluted approach at first,
but it greatly accelerated our progress afterwards.

6.
Related Work and Conclusion

In the concurrency and behavioural types communities, there is growing
interest in mechanisation and the use of proof assistants to validate
research. As a recent example,

Hinrichsen
et al. 2020
explore the notion of semantic typing using a
concurrent separation logic as a semantic domain to build on top a
language to describe binary session types. On the same vein,
SteelCore
(
Swamy et al. 2020
)
allows DSLs to take advantage of solid the
semantic foundations provided by a proof assistant. Where their
works use separation logic as a foundation,
Zooid
uses MPST and
their coinductive expansion.

The ambition of mechanisation in behavioural types is increasing and
collaborative projects that explore the space of available solutions
are an important tool for the community, where they explore different
representations of binders (names, de Bruijn indices/levels, nominals
respectively), see
(
VEST 2020
, Discussion)
. In this work we
sidetrack the question by designing
Zooid
to use a shallow
embedding of its binders (thus avoiding to need an explicit
representation for variables). In our experience, this is a simple and
valuable technique for the situations where it is applicable.

Other works also explore ideas on
binary
session types using
proof assistants and mechanised proofs. For example,

Brady 2017
develops a methodology to describe safely
communicating programs and implements DSLs, embedded in Idris,
relying on the Idris type checker.
Thiemann 2019
develops
an intrinsically typed semantics in Agda that provides preservation
and a notion of progress for binary session types.
Gay
et al. 2020

explore the interaction between duality and recursive types and how
they take advantage of mechanisation to formalise some of their
results.
Tassarotti
et al. 2017
show the correctness (in the Coq
proof assistant) of a compiler that uses an intermediate language
based on a simplified version of the GV system
(
Gay and
Vasconcelos 2010
)
to add
session types to a functional programming language. And

Orchard and
Yoshida 2015
discuss the relation between session types and
effect systems, and implement their code in the Agda proof assistant.
Their formalisation concentrates on translating between effect systems
and session types in a type preserving manner.
Castro
et al. 2019a

present a type preservation of binary session types
(
Honda
et al. 1998
;
Yoshida and
Vasconcelos 2007
)
as a case study of using their
tool
(
Castro
et al. 2020
)
. Furthermore,
Goto et al. 2016
present a
session types system with session polymorphism and use Coq to prove
type soundness of their system. Note that none of the above works on
session types treats
multiparty session types
– they are
limited to
binary
session types.

Our work on MPST uses mechanisation to both give a fresh look at trace
equivalence
(
Deniélou and
Yoshida 2013
)
in MPST and to further explore
its relation to a process calculus. At the same time our aim is to
provide a bedrock for future projects dealing with the MPST theories.
And crucially, this is the first work that tackles
a full syntax
of asynchronous multiparty session types
that type the whole
interaction, as opposed to binary session types, which only type
individual channels.

Furthermore, in this work, we present not only
Zooid
as
a certified process language, but also the methodology to design a
certified language like this. Zooid’s design starts with the theory,
then the mechanised metatheory, and, finally, implementing a deeply embedded process language (deeply embedded in
two ways: as a DSL and in the library of definitions and lemmas
provided in the proof mechanisation). We propose
Zooid
as an
alternative to writing an implementation that is proved correct post
facto. There is no tension between proofs and implementation, since
the proofs enable the
implementation. An important feature of our design is the
correspondence of
syntactic objects and their infinite tree representation
.
Coinductive trees allow us to deal smoothly with semantics and avoid
bindings: such a technique applies to languages with equi-recursion,
a widespread construct
(
Ghilezan et al. 2019
;
Pierce 2002
;
Severi and
Dezani-ciancaglini 2019
)
. On the other hand we
have kept an inductive type system for processes, so that we have
finite, easy-to-inspect, structures, on which we can make
computations. Our novel design takes advantage of the infinite-tree
representation of syntactic objects, thus providing us with
syntactic types for Zooid and coinductive representation for the
proofs.

Regarding the choice of tool and inspiration in this work, we point
out that the first objective is to mechanise trace equivalence between
global and local types. For that, we
took inspiration from more semantic representations of session
types
(
Ghilezan et al. 2019
;
Xia et al. 2019
)
. The choice of the Coq proof
assistant
(
The Coq
Development Team 2020
)
was motivated by its stability, rich
support for coinduction, and good support for the extraction of
certified code. Stability is important since this is a codebase that
we expect to work on and expand for future projects. The proofs take
advantage of small scale reflection
(
Gonthier and
Mahboubi 2010
)
using
Ssreflect to structure our development. And given the pervasive need
for greatest fixed points in MPST, we extensively use the PaCo
library
(
Hur
et al. 2013
)
for the proofs that depend on coinduction.

To conclude, we design and implement a certified language for
concurrent processes supporting MPST. We start by mechanising the
meta-theory of asynchronous MPST, and prove the soundness and
completness theorems of trace semantics of global and local types. We
then build
Zooid
, a process language on top of that. Using code
extraction, we interface with OCaml code to produce running
implementations of the processes specified in
Zooid
.

This work on mechanising MPST and
Zooid
is a founding stone, there
are many exciting opportunities for future work. On top of our
framework, we plan to explore new ideas and extensions of the theory
of session types. The immediate next step is to make the proofs
extensible, for example by allowing easy integration of custom merge
strategies, adding advanced features such as indexed dependent session
types
(
Castro
et al. 2019b
)
, timed
specifications
(
Bocchi
et al. 2014
;
Bocchi
et al. 2015
)
, or session/channel
delegation
(
Honda
et al. 2008
)
. Moreover, we intend to apply
the work in this paper (and its extensions) to implement a certified
toolchain for the Scribble protocol description language
(available at
http://www.scribble.org
), also known as “the practical
incarnation of multiparty session types”

(
Honda et al. 2011
;
Neykova and
Yoshida 2019
)
. To this
aim we plan to translate from Scribble to MPST style global types,
following the Featherweight Scribble
formalisation
(
Neykova and
Yoshida 2019
)
.

Acknowledgements.
We thank the PLDI reviewers for their careful reviews and
suggestions. We thank Fangyi Zhou for their comments and testing the
artifact. The work is supported by
Sponsor EPSRC
https://epsrc.ukri.org/
, grants
Grant #EP/T006544/1, Grant #EP/K011715/1,
Grant #EP/K034413/1, Grant #EP/L00058X/1,
Grant #EP/N027833/1, Grant #EP/N028201/1,
Grant #EP/T014709/1, and Grant #EP/V000462/1
and by Sponsor NCSS/EPSRC
Grant #VeTSS.

Appendix A
Multiparty Session Types in Coq

One of the contributions of our work is a mechanisation of multiparty session types;
this section is dedicated to their metatheory,
as we have formalised it in Coq: we follow the structure of the Coq
development, and indicate with precise pointers where definitions and
results can be found in the Coq formalisation associated to this
paper. We will present proof outlines for some of the main results, while the full proofs are to be found in the Coq formalisation associated with this paper; such outlines are meant to guide the interested reader through our formalisation.

This appendix is structured as a formalised journey towards semantics.
We first present the classic syntax of global and local session types.
We then introduce coinductive global and local trees as a “more
semantic version” of types (Sections
A.1
and

A.2
). We formalise a precise relation to associate trees
to types, and then we show that it preserves projections (Section

A.3
). This closes the square

(M.1)
of Figure
2
.
We introduce buffers to deal with asynchronicity (Section

A.4
), we then define small-step semantics
for global and local types, via labelled transition systems on trees
(Section
A.5
). Finally, in Section
A.6

we present our main result: execution trace equivalence for global and
local types, thus closing the square
(M.2)
of
Figure
2
.

A.1.
Global Types

This subsection gives definitions for global types, following our Coq
development. The literature offers a wide variety of presentations of
global types
(
Honda
et al. 2008
;
Honda
et al. 2016
;
Scalas
et al. 2019
;
Coppo et al. 2015
)
,
each exploring different aspects of communication. Building on

(
Deniélou and
Yoshida 2013
)
, we formalise
asynchronous multiparty
session types
(MPST), which allow us to capture the essential
behaviour of asynchronous message exchange, where messages are
transmitted via FIFO queues, and treat the key features of MPST
including selection, branching and recursion.

We first discuss the formalisation of
global types
, covered in
the Coq files of the folder
Global
. We use
sorts
to refer to the
types of supported message payloads, covered in the Coq file:
Common/AtomSets.v
.

Definition A.1 (Sorts and global types).

Sorts
(datatype
mty
in
Common/AtomSets.v
), ranged over by
S
,
and
global types
(datatype
g_ty
in
Global/Syntax.v
), ranged over by
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, are generated by:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}&::=&\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbin{\mathbf{|\!\!|}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{int}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbin{\mathbf{|\!\!|}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{bool}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbin{\mathbf{|\!\!|}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\texttt{+}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbin{\mathbf{|\!\!|}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\texttt{*}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbin{\mathbf{|\!\!|}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{seq}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\\[2.84526pt]
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}&::=&\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\mathbin{\mathbf{|\!\!|}}\;\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\mathbin{\mathbf{|\!\!|}}\;\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\mathbin{\mathbf{|\!\!|}}\;\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf p}\to{\sf q}:\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\end{array}

We require that
{\sf p}\neq{\sf q}
,
I\neq\emptyset,
and

\ell_{i}\neq\ell_{j}
whenever
i\neq j,
for all
i,j\in I
.

In Definition
A.1
, the type

\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf p}\to{\sf q}:\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
describes a protocol where participant
{\sf p}
must send to
{\sf q}
one message with label
\ell_{i}
and a value of sort
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}{}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
as payload, for
some
i\in I
; then, depending on which
\ell_{i}
was sent by
{\sf p}
, the protocol continues as
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}{}_{i}
. Sorts can be basic types such as
natural numbers (
nat
), integers (
int
), booleans (
bool
) or
recursive combinations of these, as sums (
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{+}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
), pairs
(
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{*}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
) or lists (
seq
). The type
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
represents a terminated protocol. Recursive protocol is modelled as
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, where recursion variable
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is bound.

The representation of the syntax above as inductive types is standard.
In Coq we represent the recursion binder using de Bruijn indices
(
de Bruijn 1972
;
Gordon 1994
;
McKinna and
Pollack 1999
;
McBride and
McKinna 2004
)
.To ease the presentation throughout the paper,
we keep using explicit names for variables.

As customary in the literature, we are interested in global types such
that (1) bound variables are
guarded
—e.g.,

\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf p}\to{\sf q}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\ell(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}).\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

is a valid global type,
whereas
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{.}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is not—and (2) types are
closed
,
i.e., all recursion variables are bound by
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
. We will mostly
leave these conditions implicit, however we provide below the two
formal definitions.

Definition A.2 (Guardedness for global types).

We say that a global type is
guarded
(
guarded
in

Global/Syntax.v
) according to the following definition:

\small\begin{array}[]{c}\dfrac{}{\texttt{guarded}\ \color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\hskip 9.24994pt\dfrac{}{\texttt{guarded}\ \color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\hskip 9.24994pt\dfrac{\texttt{not\_pure\_rec}\;\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hskip 9.24994pt\texttt{guarded}\ \color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\texttt{guarded}\ (\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}\\[8.53581pt]
\dfrac{\forall i\in I.\texttt{guarded}\ {\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}{}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}{\texttt{guarded}\ (\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf p}\to{\sf q}:\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}\end{array}

where
\texttt{not\_pure\_rec}\;\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
means that
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is different
from
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{Y_{1}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}.\dots\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{Y_{n}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and also

\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\neq\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
.

Definition A.3 (Free variables and closure (global types)).

The set of
free variables
of a global type
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
,
\texttt{fv}(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})
,
(
g_fidx
in
Global/Syntax.v
) is defined as follows.

\small\begin{array}[]{c}\texttt{fv}(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})=\emptyset,\ \texttt{fv}(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})=\{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\},\ \texttt{fv}(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})=(\texttt{fv}(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}))\backslash\{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\},\\
\texttt{fv}(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf p}\to{\sf q}:\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})=\bigcup\limits_{i\in I}\texttt{fv}({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}{}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})\end{array}

We say that
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is
closed
(
g_closed
in

Global/Syntax.v
) if it does not contain free variables, namely:

\texttt{closed}\ \color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\leftrightarrow(\texttt{fv}(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})=\emptyset)
.

We define the
set of participants of a global type
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
(
participants
in
Global/Syntax.v
), by structural induction on
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, as follows:

\small\begin{array}[]{c}\texttt{prts}\ \color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\texttt{prts}\ \color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\emptyset\hskip 9.24994pt\texttt{prts}\ \color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\texttt{prts}\ {\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
\texttt{prts}\ {\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf p}\to{\sf q}:\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}=\{{\sf p},{\sf q}\}\cup\bigcup\limits_{i\in I}\texttt{prts}\ {\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\end{array}

Participants of a global type are those roles that are involved in the
communication.

In session types, it is common practice to adopt the

equi-recursive viewpoint

(
Pierce 2002
)
, i.e., to identify

\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}/\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, given that
their intended behaviour is the same. Such unravelling of the
recursion constructor can be performed infinitely many times, thus
obtaining possibly infinite trees, whose structure derives from the
above syntax of global types
(
Ghilezan et al. 2019
)
.

In the formalisation we provide a
coinductively defined
datatype
(codatatype) of
finitely branching trees
with

possible infinite depth
. Their branching mirrors the branching
of global types into their continuations and the infinite depth allows
us to indefinitely unravel recursion.

We have a similar coinductive representation for local types
(§
A.2
). For the definitions of, and proofs about,
coinductive objects in Coq, we have taken advantage of the Paco
library
(
Hur
et al. 2013
)
for parametrised coinduction, which allows for a
more compositional reasoning in the formalisation than the standard

cofix
construction.

Definition A.4 (Semantic global trees).

Semantic global trees
(datatypes
rg_ty
and
ig_ty
in

Global/Tree.v
, see Remark
A.6
), ranged over by
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, are terms generated
coinductively

by:

\begin{array}[]{rll}\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}&::=&\\
&&\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\mathtt{end}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\mathbin{\mathbf{|\!\!|}}\;\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf p}\to{\sf q}:\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\\
&&\;\mathbin{\mathbf{|\!\!|}}\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf p}\mathrel{\hbox to12.19pt{\vbox to13.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.09431pt\lower-8.3694pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

We require that
{\sf p}\neq{\sf q}
,
I\neq\emptyset,
and

\ell_{i}\neq\ell_{j}
whenever
i\neq j,
for all
i,j\in I
.

The above codatatype represents the bridge between the syntax and the
semantics for global types.
Here we make explicit the two asynchronous stages of the communication of a
message:

•

\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf p}\to{\sf q}:\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
represents the status where a
message from the participant
{\sf p}
to the participant
{\sf q}
has yet to
be sent;

•

\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf p}\mathrel{\hbox to12.19pt{\vbox to13.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.09431pt\lower-8.3694pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{

represents the status
immediately after the above: the label
\ell_{j}
has been picked
among the
\ell_{i}
,
{\sf p}
has sent the message, with payload
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}{}_{j}
,
but
{\sf q}
has not received it yet.

We can now give the central definition of coinductive unravelling for
global types, which relates a global type with its semantic tree.

Definition A.5 (Global unravelling).

Unravelling of global types
(definition
GUnroll
in

Global/Unravel.v
) is the relation between global types and semantic
global trees coinductively defined by:

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5469}{\DefTirNameOld{\footnotesize[{g-unr-end}]}}&\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5469}{\DefTirNameOld{\footnotesize[{g-unr-rec}]}}\\
\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\dfrac{}{\phantom{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\Re\;\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\mathtt{end}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}$\cr\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\dfrac{\phantom{}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\Re\;\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\mathtt{end}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}$}\crcr}}&\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\dfrac{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}/\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\Re\;\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\phantom{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\Re\;\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}$\cr\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\dfrac{\phantom{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}/\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\Re\;\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\Re\;\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}$}\crcr}}&\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{l}\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5469}{\DefTirNameOld{\footnotesize[{g-unr-msg}]}}\\
\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\dfrac{\forall i\in I.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}{}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\Re\;\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\phantom{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf p}\to{\sf q}:\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\Re\;\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf p}\to{\sf q}:\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}$\cr\raisebox{-2.0pt}{$\dfrac{\phantom{\forall i\in I.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}{}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\Re\;\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf p}\to{\sf q}:\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\Re\;\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf p}\to{\sf q}:\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}$}\crcr}}\end{array}

The unravelling operation gives us a snapshot of all possible
executions of the global type. As infinite trees, both

\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}/\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
have the same
representation: we are able to identify global types that are the same
up to unfolding, thus offering a rigorous behavioural
characterisation.
Moreover, we have obtained a
binding-free syntax for global types and therefore removed one of the
most notoriously tedious features of formal reasoning.

Remark A.6.

In Coq the coinductive datatype
for global trees is defined in a slightly different way, in
particular we formally split Definition
A.4
in
two parts. First, we define its coinductive core as follows
(datatype
rg_ty
in
Global.v

(datatype

is now part only of the syntax for inductive

In Coq, distinguishing the two (co)datatypes and injecting one into
the other, has allowed us to perform “induction on global trees” (on
their prefixes), by considering each time the
finite number of
unevaluated steps
, namely the number of messages that have been
sent and not yet received. About this, we notice that formally there
is no isomorphism between the codatatype of infinite trees in
Definition
A.4
and the one we obtain by composing
prefixes of Definition
A.8
and trees of
Definition
A.7
: the constructor

can now appear only inside the
inductive prefix. However, this does not affect the unravelling
operation
\Re
(Definition
A.5
), since types are
unravelled in trees without any construct
