\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}{\sf q}:\{\ell_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}}_{i}).\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}{}^{\prime}\upharpoonright\!\upharpoonright(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime})
).

The proof above requires “compatibility” and “synchronisation” lemmas, e.g., to make sure that when we build
(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime})
from the different
(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime}_{i})
, we obtain exactly the result of applying
run_step
to
(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)
.

(3)
Lastly we need to prove
(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)\xrightarrow{a}(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime})
, and proceed by induction on

\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xrightarrow{a}\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
. Here we need to show that if
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
performs a step with the action
a
, then its one-shot projection
(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)

is able to perform a step
with the same action
a
; then we now that this step will be performed
(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime})
, which has been defined via
run_step
exactly with this purpose. In
Local/Semantics.v
we define a predicate,
runnable : env * qenv
\rightarrow
bool
, that formalises the concept that an environment is able to perform a step, returning
true
or
false
accordingly. Thus we conclude, by proving the next results:

•

if
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xrightarrow{a}\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\upharpoonright\!\upharpoonright(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)
then
\texttt{runnable}\ (\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)
(lemma
local_runnable
in
TraceEquiv.v
);

•

if
\texttt{runnable}\ (\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)
then
(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)\xrightarrow{a}(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime})
, where
(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime})=\texttt{run\_step}\ (\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)
(lemma
run_step_sound
in
Local/Semantics.v
).

∎

Dually, we prove completeness for step semantics on trees. The
intuition is the same as for soundness, but reading Figures

12(a)
and
12(b)
from bottom to top: each time
a local tree in the environment performs a step, the global tree also
performs one.

Theorem A.34 (Step Completeness).

(Theorem

Project_lstep
in
TraceEquiv.v
)

If
(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)\xrightarrow{a}(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime})
and

\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\upharpoonright\!\upharpoonright(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)
, there exist
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
such that

\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\upharpoonright\!\upharpoonright(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime})
and

\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xrightarrow{a}\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
.

Proof Outline.

The proof structure is the following (again, the intuition for the base cases is carried by Figure
12
):

(1)

we prove that exists
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
such that
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xrightarrow{a}\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
;

(2)

we prove that for this very
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
it must hold that
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\upharpoonright\!\upharpoonright(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime})
.

(1)
is taken care of by lemma
Project_gstep
in
TraceEquiv.v
. The proof of such lemma proceed by induction on the prefix of the global tree
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
(see Remark
A.6
). The case for
\color[rgb]{0.4336,0.3047,0.2148}{\mathtt{inj}^{\sf p}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is outsourced to the lemma
CProj_step
in
TraceEquiv.v
. The induction cases in lemma
Project_gstep
, including the one handled by
CProj_step
, are all solved thanks to a—quite tedious—combination of case analysis and inversion lemmas about projections (collected in the lemma
Project_inv

Projection/CProject.v
).

The proof for
(2)
is more interesting. The goal itself is handled by
Project_gstep_proj
in
TraceEquiv.v
. First we observe that, given
(1)
, namely
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xrightarrow{a}\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, and the hypothesis
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\upharpoonright\!\upharpoonright(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)
, we know that for
(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime\prime})=\texttt{run\_step}\ (\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)
it holds that
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\upharpoonright\!\upharpoonright(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime\prime})
(see proof of Theorem
3.16
and lemma
runstep_proj
in
TraceEquiv.v
). Then we observe that, again by case analysis and inversion, we can prove lemma
lstep_eq
in
Local/Semantics.v
:

\text{If}\quad(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)\xrightarrow{a}(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime})\quad\text{and}\quad(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)\xrightarrow{a}(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime\prime})\text{,}\quad\text{then}\quad(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime\prime})=(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime\prime})\text{.}

We conclude by lemma
run_step_compl
in
Local/Semantics.v
, that combines the above result with lemma
run_step_sound
in
Local/Semantics.v
. Indeed this guarantees that the hypothesis
\quad(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)\xrightarrow{a}(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime\prime})
in
lstep_eq
above is satisfied (remember that we have chosen
(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime\prime})=\texttt{run\_step}\ (\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)
; see again the proof for the soundness, Theorem
A.33
).
∎

A.6.
Trace Semantics and Trace Equivalence

To conclude the presentation of the metatheory we show trace
equivalence for global and local types. The end result is the Coq
formalisation of an adaptation of Theorem 3.1 in

(
Deniélou and
Yoshida 2013
)
to our definition of semantics via
coinductive trees.

Traces are defined simply as streams of actions.

Definition A.35 (Traces).

(Codatatype
trace
in
Common/Action.v
), ranged
over by
t
, are terms generated
coinductively
by

\ \ t\ \ \ ::=\quad[]\;\mathbin{\mathbf{|\!\!|}}\;{a}\#{t}\quad
where
a

is either a sending action
!{\sf p}{\sf q}(\ell,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})
or a receiving one

?{\sf q}{\sf p}(\ell,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{S}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})
(§
A.5
). We use the same notation
as for lists, however we bare in mind that this definiton is
coinductive, hence it generates possibly infinite streams.

We associate traces to the execution of global and local trees.

Definition A.36 (Admissible traces for a global
tree).

We say that a trace is admissible for a global tree if the
coinductive relation
\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{g}}\;{\_}\;{\_}
(definition
g_lts
in

Global/Semantics.v
) holds:

\dfrac{}{\phantom{\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{g}}\;{[]}\;{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\mathtt{end}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}}

\dfrac{\phantom{}}{\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{g}}\;{[]}\;{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\mathtt{end}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}

\dfrac{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xrightarrow{a}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\quad\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{g}}\;{t}\;{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}{\phantom{\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{g}}\;{{a}\#{t}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}}

\dfrac{\phantom{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\xrightarrow{a}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\quad\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{g}}\;{t}\;{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}{}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}}{\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{g}}\;{{a}\#{t}}\;{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}

For local trees, we consider the whole protocol, namely the pair of
local and queue environments.

Definition A.37 (Admissible traces for environments).

We say that a trace is admissible for a pair of a local environment
and a queue environment if the coinductive relation
\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{l}}\;{\_}\;{\_}

(definition
l_lts
in
Local/Semantics.v
) holds:

\dfrac{\forall{\sf p}.\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ {\sf p}=\texttt{None}}{\phantom{\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{l}}\;{[]}\;{(}\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\epsilon)}}

\dfrac{\phantom{\forall{\sf p}.\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ {\sf p}=\texttt{None}}}{\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{l}}\;{[]}\;{(}\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\epsilon)}

\dfrac{(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)\xrightarrow{a}(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime})\quad\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{l}}\;{t}\;{(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime})}}{\phantom{\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{l}}\;{{a}\#{t}}\;{(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)}}}

\dfrac{\phantom{(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)\xrightarrow{a}(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime})\quad\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{l}}\;{t}\;{(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q^{\prime})}}}{\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{l}}\;{{a}\#{t}}\;{(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)}}

Observe that, given the element of non-determinism in our semantics
(see §
A.5
), generally more than one execution trace
are admissible for a global tree (or for an environment).

We can now state and prove the
trace equivalence
theorem for
multiparty session types.

Theorem A.38 (Trace equivalence).

(Theorem

TraceEquivalence
in
TraceEquiv.v
.)

If
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\upharpoonright\!\upharpoonright(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)
then

\ \texttt{tr}^{\texttt{g}}\;{t}\;{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\

if and only if
\ \texttt{tr}^{\texttt{l}}\;{t}\;{(}\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)\
.

Proof Sketch.

(Theorem
TraceEquivalence
in
TraceEquiv.v
.)

(If)
We assume
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\upharpoonright\!\upharpoonright(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)
and
\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{g}}\;{t}\;{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

and we proceed by coinduction (exploiting the techniques from the
Paco library
(
Hur
et al. 2013
)
) on the
\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{l}}\;{\_}\;{\_}
relation in the goal,
followed by a case analysis on
\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{g}}\;{\_}\;{\_}
in hypothesis. The base

[]
case is handled simply by inversion lemmas, while the
coinductive one is solved thanks to the soundness theorem (Theorem

A.33
).

(Only If)
We assume
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\upharpoonright\!\upharpoonright(\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)
and

\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{l}}\;{t}\;{(}\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},Q)
. Again we proceed by coinduction (again
exploiting the Paco techniques
(
Hur
et al. 2013
)
) on the
\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{g}}\;{\_}\;{\_}

relation in the goal, followed by a case analysis on
\texttt{tr}^{\texttt{l}}\;{\_}\;{\_}

in hypothesis. The base
[]
case is handled simply by inversion
lemmas, while the coinductive one is solved by the completeness
result (Theorem
A.34
).
∎

The above result concludes our formalisation effort of the metatheory
of multiparty session types, from their syntactic specification to the
equivalence of global and local semantics. We have built the
formalisation of the type-related part of
the diagram
: squares

(M.1)
and
(M.2)
in Figure

2
.

\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\sf G}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{\sf G}^{\sf c}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
global trace
\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
\color[rgb]{0.332,0.418,0.1836}{{\sf L^{c}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
local trace
(M.1)
(M.2)
\Re
LTS
\Re
LTS
\upharpoonright
\upharpoonright^{\textsf{c}}
=

Appendix B
Process extraction

This function, available in
Proc.v
, translates a
Zooid
process into a monadic value.

⬇

Section

.

:

:

.

ProcExtraction
.

The translation is defined recursively on the structure of processes,
and it constructs a sequence of monadic actions using bind
connecting each action to its continuation.

B.1.
Constructing a Recursive Ping-pong Client

We present now several examples implementing the clients of a ping-pong server.
The global protocol that describes the behaviour of all these participants is:

\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.8}{Definition}}\;\texttt{ping\_pong}:=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mu\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}.}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\sf Alice}}\to{{\sf Bob}}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ \{\\
\quad\ell_{1}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{unit}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\;\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0};\;\ \ell_{2}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\sf Bob}}\to{{\sf Alice}}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell_{3}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\}.\end{array}

Here,
{{\sf Alice}}
acts as the client for
{{\sf Bob}}
, which is the ping-pong server.

{{\sf Alice}}
can send zero or more
ping
messages (label
\ell_{2}
), and
finally quitting (label
\ell_{1}
).
{{\sf Bob}}
, for each ping received, will reply a

pong
message (label
\ell_{3}
).

Just as in the
pipeline
example, we project
ping_pong
, and get the local type
for
{{\sf Alice}}
:
alice
lt
. We define several different implementations of

alice
lt
adhering to the protocol specification. The first client,

\texttt{alice}_{0}
simply quits without sending any ping. To be able to typecheck it
against
alice
lt
, we need to specify the missing labels in the process
specification:

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.8}{Definition}}\;\texttt{alice}_{0}:\texttt{wt\_proc}\;\texttt{alice${}_{lt}$}:=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{loop}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
\ \begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{select}\;{{\sf Bob}}\;\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}[\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{otherwise}\Rightarrow\ell_{1},\;\texttt{tt}:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{unit}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\texttt{!}\;}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{finish}}\\
\mid\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{skip}\Rightarrow\ell_{2},\;\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\texttt{!}\;}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{?[{{{\sf Bob}}}];}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\ell_{3}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0});\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}])\end{array}}\end{array}\end{array}

The
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{select}\;{{\sf Bob}}\;}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
construct specifies that the default branch is to send

\ell_{1}
, and then finish, and that the unimplemented behaviour is to send

\ell_{2}
and a
nat
, and then receiving
\ell_{3}
from
{{\sf Bob}}
, and then
jumping to
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{loop}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
. Similarly, we define the process that
keeps sending
\ell_{2}
to
{{\sf Bob}}
:

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.8}{Definition}}\;\texttt{alice}_{1}:\texttt{wt\_proc}\;\texttt{alice${}_{lt}$}:=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{loop}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\\
\ \begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{select}\;{{\sf Bob}}\;\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}[\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{skip}\Rightarrow\ell_{1},\;\texttt{tt}\texttt{!}\;}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{end}}\\
\mid\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{otherwise}\Rightarrow\ell_{2},\;0:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\texttt{!}\;}\\
\ \color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{recv}\;{{\sf Bob}}\;(\ell_{3},\;x:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0})\texttt{?}\;}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{jump}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}])\end{array}}\end{array}\end{array}

Since
Proc
and the local types are inductively defined, there will be
sometimes valid processes with a local type that is not
exactly
the
projection of a participant in the global type. In such cases, we require proofs
that the local type of the process is equal up to unravelling to the local type
projected from the global type. For example,
\texttt{alice}_{0}
could be defined
without using
loop
, by providing the local type that results of
unravelling once
alice
lt
:

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.8}{Definition}}\;\texttt{alice}_{3}:\texttt{typed\_proc}:=\\
\ \begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}[\texttt{proc}\\
\ \color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{select}\;{{\sf Bob}}\;\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}[\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{otherwise}\Rightarrow\ell_{1},\;\texttt{tt}:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{unit}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\texttt{!}\;}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{finish}}\\
\mid\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{skip}\Rightarrow\ell_{2},\;\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\texttt{!}\;}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{?[{{{\sf Bob}}}];}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\ell_{3}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0});\texttt{alice${}_{lt}$}]]\end{array}}\end{array}\end{array}

The type
typed_proc
is the dependent pair type:
\{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbin{\&}\texttt{wt\_proc}\;\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\}
. The notation
[\texttt{proc}\;\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{Z}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]
is defined as:

\texttt{existT}\;(\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}{\texttt{fun}\;\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}\Rightarrow\texttt{wt\_proc}\;\;\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{{\sf L}}\color[rgb]{0.78,0.08,0.52}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})\;\_\;\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{Z}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
.

The underscore
\_

is inferred by Coq, since
Zooid
constructs fully determine their local type
from the inputs.
The first projection
\texttt{projT1}\;\texttt{alice}_{3}
is the inferred local
type. To ensure that
\texttt{alice}_{3}
behaves as prescribed by
ping_pong
, we
need to prove that its inferred local type is equal to
alice
lt
up to
unravelling. But for this example, it is enough to unfold
alice
lt
once, and
compare the result syntactically with
\texttt{projT1}\;\texttt{alice}_{3}
. Similarly, if we define a process that sends a fixed number of

n
pings and then finishes, we would need to prove that its local type is
syntactically equal to the
n
-th unfolding of
alice
lt
, which can be done
simply by evaluating its comparison.

Suppose now that we wish to implement a client that sends an undefined number of
pings, until the server replies a natural number greater than some
k
. We show
below the
Zooid
specification:

\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\texttt{\color[rgb]{0.6,0,0.8}{Definition}}\;\texttt{alice}_{4}:\texttt{typed\_proc}:=[\texttt{proc}\\
\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{select}\;{{\sf Bob}}\;\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}[\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{skip}\Rightarrow\ell_{1},\;\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{unit}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\texttt{!}\;}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{end}}\\
\mid\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{otherwise}\Rightarrow\ell_{2},\;0:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\texttt{!}\;}\\
\ \ \color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{loop}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\;(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{recv}\;{{\sf Bob}}\;(\ell_{3},\;x:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0})\texttt{?}\;}\\
\qquad\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{select}\;{{\sf Bob}}\;\begin{array}[t]{@{}l@{}}[\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{case}\;x\geq k\Rightarrow\ell_{1},\;\texttt{tt}:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{unit}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\texttt{!}\;}\\
\ \ \color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{finish}}\\
\mid\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{otherwise}\Rightarrow\ell_{2},\;x:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\texttt{!}\;}\\
\ \ \color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}{\texttt{jump}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}])]]\end{array}}\end{array}}\end{array}\end{array}

The local type for
\texttt{alice}_{4}
is not syntactically equal to
alice
lt
:

\begin{array}[]{l}\texttt{alice${}_{lt}$}=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mu\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}.\;\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{![{{{\sf Bob}}}];}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\{\ell_{1}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{unit}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0};\\
\quad\ell_{2}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{?[{{{\sf Bob}}}];}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell_{3}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\}\par\\[5.0pt]
\texttt{projT1}\;\texttt{alice}_{4}=\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{![{{{\sf Bob}}}];}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\{\ \ell_{1}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{unit}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0};\;\ell_{2}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mu\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}.\\
\quad\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{?[{{{\sf Bob}}}];}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ell_{3}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{![{{{\sf Bob}}}];}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\{\ell_{1}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{unit}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0};\;\ell_{2}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\texttt{nat}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}).\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}.\}\}\par\end{array}

However, a simple proof by coinduction can show
that both types unravel to the same
local tree.
